### 2026-10-01 — Task 21 Phase 4: grace, expiry and the upgrade — COMPLETE

- **The banner is derived, non-dismissible, and silent for a free account.** `subscription_banner.dart` computes one of four states from the cached expiry and the recorded clock offset (`resolveSubscriptionBanner`, pure and unit-tested): **none** (active, or never subscribed — no expiry means no nag), **grace** (past expiry but inside 48h: *"Your subscription expired. Renew to avoid losing sync access."*), **expiredOwner** (*"Your premium subscription has ended. Renew your subscription to restore sync."*), and **expiredAssociate** (*"Your firm's premium subscription has ended. Contact your firm owner to renew."*) — the associate/owner split is `firmMember && role != owner`, because an associate's entitlement is the owner's. It is deliberately **not** dismissible: a renewal warning the user swiped away is one they never saw. Mounted in the same two hosts the conflict banner uses (`AppShellRoot` and `CasesScreenModal`), above the notice and conflict strips; it renders `SizedBox.shrink()` when all is well. Since the sync gate already refuses to run below the derived entitlement, "sync disabled immediately after grace, no data deleted" needed no new code — this is the visible half.
- **Activation detection is durable, because the upgrade can happen while the app is closed.** `SubscriptionActivation.observe(premium)` stores the last observed entitlement per account (`subscription_was_premium_<userId>`) and runs `SyncService.runBacklogPush()` exactly when a stored `false` becomes `true`. A missing value is *no prior state* — a first-ever launch stores the value and never pushes, so a new install cannot fire a backlog pass — and a stored `false` that reads `true` on the next launch is the approval landing during the profile refresh (Task 18's backlog pass had no production caller until now). Wired into `sync_trigger_scope.dart`'s existing `premiumAccessProvider` listener, so the runtime transition and the launch/resume refresh both flow through one path. **A Broadcast ping is not used and cannot be:** `profiles`/`payment_submissions` carry no row trigger, so the plan's "Broadcast ping **or** profile refresh" is satisfied by the refresh.
- **Manage Account now shows the plan and the expiry, in `DD/MM/YYYY`.** Behind Subscription Details (the existing sheet), a **Plan** row names the paid plan from the latest **approved** submission and a dash when there is none (the Subscription row already says Free/Premium, so a repeated tier word would be noise), and an **Expiry** row renders the expiry in the active locale's `DD/MM/YYYY` (or a dash). The sheet body was already made scrollable in Phase 2 for the history.
- **Tests:** 15 new — 6 in `test/features/subscription_banner_test.dart` (active/none, no-expiry, grace, past-grace owner, past-grace associate + owner, and the clock-skew flip) plus 4 widget cases (each message rendered, nothing when active) and 5 in `test/features/subscription_activation_test.dart` (first launch stores without pushing, a stored false→true pushes once, an upgrade across a restart is detected, a lapse and re-upgrade pushes again, and state is per account). One regression surfaced and was fixed: the new Plan row duplicated the tier word already in the Subscription row, so two `manage_account_test.dart` expectations matched twice; the Plan row now shows a dash without an approved submission, and the suite is green. Full `flutter test` **1239 passed** (1198 → 1239); `flutter analyze` clean; ledger `--check` clean; `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.336`.
- **Resume point:** Task 21 is feature-complete across all four phases. Remaining: run the task file's acceptance checklist (including the Rev 2.3/2.4 Addendum) item by item, and the server-side payment submission & approval test per the Verification Plan — the admin `approve_payment` path is already covered by `supabase/tests/rpc_smoke_test.sql` from Task 16, with the same one-live-account limitation Task 20 recorded (the two-real-account end-to-end run is exercised in a rolled-back transaction instead).

### 2026-10-01 — Task 21 Phase 3: one derivation, now skew-aware — COMPLETE

- **Items 1–3 were already true, and were verified rather than rewritten.** `resolvePremiumAccess` in `premium_access.dart` has been the single real derivation (`subscription_expiry + 48h grace > now`, never `subscription_status`) since Task 15, and it already inherits the firm's mirrored `firms.subscription_expiry` for a member (Task 16's mirror) and already feeds the three gates with no call-site change: the sync worker (`SyncService._isEntitled`), firm-mode gating (`ensure_firm`'s premium gate via `premiumAccessProvider`) and the 50-case Add Case gate (`add_edit_case_sheet` / `resolvePremiumAccess`). Item 5 (lapsed premium keeps everything) is untouched — the gate only refuses *adding* past 50.
- **Item 4 is the only change, and it is deliberately local.** The derivation now reads the recorded clock offset (Task 18 Phase 3's `sync_metadata.clock_offset_ms`, stored as `server - device`) and corrects the device clock before comparing, so a device running ahead cannot falsely expire a subscription still inside its 48h grace window. An offset of 0 (no successful sync yet) is a no-op, so every existing call site and test is unchanged, and an explicit `now` is treated as device time and corrected the same way. The RLS/`push_changes` server checks remain the security boundary — this only reduces false *banners*.
- **Tests:** 7 new in `test/features/premium_access_test.dart` over an in-memory database — unexpired, in-grace (1h past expiry), past grace (49h), display-only `premium` status cannot grant access, no expiry, firm-inherited (member's own expiry 30 days lapsed but the firm's active), and the skew case (a device 2h ahead where 49h-by-device is 47h-by-server, so the offset flips it from false to true). `flutter analyze` clean; no SQL this phase. Version `0.0.335`.
- **Resume point:** Task 21 Phase 4 — the 48h grace-period banner and the two post-grace banners (owner/associate), activated on the existing banner hosts; activation detection via the launch/resume profile refresh (a `profiles`/`payment_submissions` Broadcast ping does not exist for these tables) calling `runBacklogPush()` on a `false → true` entitlement transition; and plan + expiry (`DD/MM/YYYY`) + status reflected in Manage Account. Then the task file's acceptance checklist.

### 2026-10-01 — Task 21 Phase 2: the checkout submits, remembers and limits — COMPLETE

- **One write path, and it never touches the server's authority.** `SupabasePaymentRepository.submit` INSERTs one own `payment_submissions` row (RLS: `user_id = auth.uid()`) and deliberately omits `submitted_at` — the server's `BEFORE INSERT` trigger assigns it, so a client value is never sent. `status` stays the server default. A `PostgrestException` is mapped to a typed `PaymentSubmitException` — the trigger's `payment_submission_limit_reached` becomes `limitReached`, a `23505` becomes `duplicateTransaction`, anything else `other`; anything non-PostgREST is `network`. `UnavailablePaymentRepository` reports every submit as `network` for a no-backend build rather than pretending a queue.
- **The draft is the point: it survives a failure and is cleared only on a confirmed success.** `PaymentDraftNotifier` keys `payment_draft_<userId>` in `SharedPreferences` (`PaymentDraft` = plan, method, phone, TrxID), saves on every keystroke through the two controller listeners, and restores in `initState`. The view model clears it and records the local stamp **only after** the repository returned without throwing; a refusal leaves both untouched. Per-user keys mean one account's half-typed TrxID never appears in another's form (Addendum: checkout drafts are never firm-shared).
- **The local 3-per-24h check is UX, and the code says so.** `PaymentSubmissionLogNotifier` keeps accepted-submission epoch stamps per user; `localSubmissionsInWindow`/`isLocallyRateLimited` are pure so the rule is unit-tested. The screen checks them before submitting to save a round trip, and the server's trigger remains authoritative — a `limitReached` refusal is worded with the same friendly sentence. A reached local limit shows *"You've reached today's submission limit. Please try again tomorrow."*; a duplicate TrxID gets its own sentence.
- **The view model owns the phase, the screen owns the wording.** `PaymentViewModel.submit` walks `idle → submitting → success/failure`, refuses an unknown plan and a signed-out caller without a request, and invalidates `myPaymentSubmissionsProvider` on success so the history refreshes. Failure kinds map to the task's exact sentences: *"Submission failed. Tap Retry."* for network/other, the limit sentence for the trigger, and a sign-in sentence when nobody is signed in.
- **History lives behind Subscription Details in Manage Account.** Phase 2 item 4, placement confirmed with the user: the sheet gained a `Payment History` section over `myPaymentSubmissionsProvider` (own rows only under RLS), each row showing the plan, the amount in the active locale's digits, a status pill (pending/approved/rejected) and the reviewer's `admin_notes`. The sheet body became scrollable so an arbitrarily long history cannot overflow it; the mockup-bound Manage Account list needed no new row.
- **Tests:** 9 new — 7 in `test/features/payment_submission_test.dart` (the two pure rate-limit cases, the per-user draft save/clear, and four view-model paths: success clears draft + records the stamp, a network failure keeps the draft, the limit trigger maps to `limitReached`, an unknown plan sends nothing) and 2 in `test/features/checkout_screen_test.dart` (a resolved number is displayed and a saved draft pre-fills the form; entering details saves the draft, a submit reaches the repository and a confirmed success clears it). New ARB keys (`payments_errorPhone/Trx`, `payments_submitFailed`, `payments_limitReached`, `payments_duplicateTrx`, `payments_submitSuccess`, `payments_historyTitle`, `payments_noSubmissions`, `payments_status*`, `payments_notesLabel`), EN + BN key-for-key, `flutter gen-l10n` re-run. `flutter analyze` clean; no SQL this phase, so the ledger is untouched. Version `0.0.334`.
- **Resume point:** Task 21 Phase 3 — derived premium entitlement: verify the existing single `subscription_expiry + 48h grace` derivation already feeds the sync worker gate, firm-mode gating and the 50-case gate with no call-site change, then apply the recorded clock-skew offset (`sync_metadata`) to the local UX check, with unit tests for active/in-grace/lapsed/firm-inherited. Then Phase 4 (grace/expiry banners, activation backlog push, Manage Account plan/expiry/status).

### 2026-10-01 — Task 21 Phase 1: the paywall sells, and the checkout reads its numbers from the server — COMPLETE

- **The paywall is out of stub limbo, and the mockup was updated with it.** Task 15's paywall matched `mockup_html_screens/paywall/code.html`, whose plan actions were disabled "Coming Soon" buttons and whose note said subscriptions were not yet available. Task 21 calls the screen `[Design-deferred]` with no mockup, but a mockup exists — the *stub's* — so the user chose to keep its layout and enable the actions. Both `Subscribe` buttons are now enabled and push the checkout with the plan as a query parameter (`?plan=monthly|annual`); `paywall_comingSoon` and `paywall_note` were removed from the ARB and the mockup HTML was updated to the enabled design, so AGENTS rule 9 still holds. The prices stay aliases of the new shared plan constants.
- **The receiving numbers are configuration, not code (Addendum / Rev 2.4 §5).** A new read-only server table `payment_configuration` (`provider` in `bkash|nagad|rocket`, `receiving_number`, `is_active`, `updated_at`) carries them: authenticated SELECT only, **no client write policy**, `receiving_number <> ''` and the provider allow-list so a malformed row cannot exist. It is deliberately **not seeded** — the real MFS numbers are not invented — and the client falls back to validated build defaults (`--dart-define PAYMENTS_<PROVIDER>_NUMBER`) until an admin inserts a row, at which point the remote value is authoritative and rotates the number without a release. Applied live to `lsthasywrdrhuuxnykau` (28 versions recorded), ledger regenerated (589 KB) with the `0.0.333` group heading, both hard-coded migration counts moved 27 → 28 and `versions.last` → `20261001000000`.
- **`PaymentsConfigurationService` is the one place a number is read, validated and retained.** Remote first, validated build defaults second. `isValidBdMobileNumber` (the one BD format rule, also used for the sender phone) and an unsupported-provider check reject a bad row without letting it replace a good default; an unreachable-but-empty table keeps the defaults; a transient refresh failure returns the last validated set marked stale (the `AsyncNotifier` hands the previous value back to `load`). Values are display-only and never logged; a widget only ever renders what the service returned.
- **Checkout is a screen, not a stub.** The approved Justice Ledger layout: a plan-summary card with the exact ৳100/৳1,000 amount, a transfer card listing each resolved provider number with copy-to-clipboard, and a payment-details card (method dropdown, sender phone, TrxID) with a Submit action. Read-only for now — submission, draft, rate-limit UX and history are Phase 2 — but the form, the copy button and the resolved numbers are real, and an empty/unavailable configuration renders an honest message rather than a blank form.
- **The Go Premium entry is a recorded mockup departure.** Settings → General gains a `Go Premium` row (hidden once the account is premium) that opens the paywall, because the task requires a Settings action and the mockup-bound Settings body has no slot for it. This is the same kind of departure Task 20 recorded for the Delete Requests entry, and it is noted in the task file.
- **Tests:** 8 new — 7 in `test/features/payments_configuration_test.dart` (valid/invalid numbers, provider allow-list, build-default fallback, remote rotation, malformed/unsupported row rejection, empty-table behaviour, retention on a transient failure with and without a previous set, and the honest empty configuration) — plus the paywall suite updated to pin enabled actions and the removed note. Targeted `test/supabase/` + l10n + payments suites **97 passed**; `flutter analyze` clean; ledger `--check` clean; `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.333`.
- **Resume point:** Task 21 Phase 2 — submission, local draft and rate limiting: `PaymentRepository` writing an own `payment_submissions` row (the client never supplies `submitted_at`), the real `PaymentViewModel`, a per-user checkout draft in `SharedPreferences` (save on keystroke, cleared only on confirmed success), the best-effort 3-per-24h UX check with the server trigger's friendly rejection copy, and own-row submission history in Manage Account. Then Phase 3 (entitlement verification + clock-skew offset) and Phase 4 (grace/expiry banners, activation backlog push, Manage Account plan/expiry/status).

### 2026-10-01 — Task 20 follow-up: RLS refuses the write the RPC already refuses — COMPLETE

- **The residual the audit flagged is closed at the policy layer, by the user's choice of scope.** `0.0.330` hardened `sync_can_write`, which is the path the *app* uses. The table policies still said `user_id = auth.uid()` on a firm-scoped row, and Supabase grants `authenticated` table-level write privileges by default, so the same removed associate could PATCH their authored firm rows straight through PostgREST — with the RPC, its version check and its receipts never involved. Worse than an edit: `cases_update_scoped`'s `with check` asked only that the NEW row keep `user_id = auth.uid()`, so a removed member could detach a firm record by nulling `firm_id`. Asked how far to go, the user chose **policies only, no grant changes**, so the direct path is now refused *by policy* rather than made unreachable by revoked privileges.
- **`20260930220000_rls_firm_membership.sql` states the same rule as the closure, in the same places.** Every INSERT and UPDATE policy that trusts authorship now requires the row to be personal (`firm_id is null`) or still the caller's firm (`firm_id is not distinct from public.current_firm_id()`) — the ten tables that share one policy shape (`cases`, `case_history`, `notes`, `case_references`, the six reference tables) are handled by one loop so their spellings cannot drift, with `conflict_log` (own rows only, no `can_edit_firm` disjunct) and `delete_requests` (whose author column is `requested_by`) written out by hand. `using` and `with check` say the same thing, which is what stops a row being updated *into* a state its writer could not have created. `can_edit_firm` / `can_delete_firm` are unchanged, so teammates and reviewers keep their access, and a `firm_id`-less personal row is untouched — that is the individual diary AGENTS rule 3 protects.
- **Two limits, both deliberate and both recorded in the migration.** SELECT policies are left alone: they compare the row's firm to the caller's with a plain equality, and a NULL on either side is not true, so a caller with no firm already reads nothing from a firm row — `is not distinct from` would be equivalent there, and rewriting a read policy for no behavioural gain is risk without reward. Table privileges are left alone by the user's decision; the migration says so plainly, including that revoking them would have closed the direct path too.
- **Applied live and proven on `lsthasywrdrhuuxnykau`, in both directions.** Before the apply, `has_table_privilege('authenticated','public.cases','UPDATE')` was true and the with-check read `(user_id = auth.uid()) OR can_edit_firm(firm_id)`; after it, `pg_get_expr` shows the membership condition on `cases`, `courts`, `conflict_log` and `delete_requests`, with the privilege deliberately unchanged. `supabase/tests/push_changes_smoke_test.sql` gained a rolled-back section that stops using the RPC and acts as the `authenticated` role instead, so RLS is evaluated rather than the owner's privileges: the removed member's direct PATCH of the firm row they authored updates **zero** rows, a direct INSERT into the firm fails the `WITH CHECK` (an error rather than an empty result, so both shapes are pinned), their pending firm request is likewise out of reach — and the owner's own direct edit still lands, so a policy that refused everything could not pass the section. Then the whole smoke run printed `SMOKE OK`, and the new assertions were proven to bite by inverting one on a scratch copy, which failed with exactly that message.
- **Guards:** `test/supabase/schema_contract_test.dart` gained a structural check on the new file (the membership comparison present, no NULL-prone equality, all ten loop tables named, `conflict_log` and `delete_requests` present, the reviewer disjuncts intact, no `for select`), and the two hard-coded migration counts moved 26 → 27 with `versions.last` now `20260930220000`.
- **Tests:** full `flutter test` **1198 passed** (1197 → 1198); `test/supabase/` 78 passed; `flutter analyze` clean; ledger `--check` clean at 27 migrations / 585 KB; `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.332`.
- **Resume point:** Task 20 is now closed on both sides of the membership rule, so the next step is the task file's acceptance checklist (phases + Addendum) or Task 21 directly — whose Phase 1 opens with the two `[Design-deferred]` screens (Paywall, Checkout) that need layout confirmation before any code, and whose Phase 4 end-to-end approval test still faces the one-live-account constraint that kept `rpc_smoke_test.sql` from running.

### 2026-10-01 — Task 20 Phase 3 item 3 closed: the requester can edit and withdraw their own pending request — COMPLETE

- **The last audit gap was a missing surface, not a missing rule.** Phase 3 item 3 ("the requester can edit/cancel their own pending request") had been satisfied only at the data layer since `0.0.326` — `refreshSnapshot`, `cancel` and `watchMine` all existed and worked — but the requester's list rows were deliberately non-interactive (`onTap: null`), so there was no way to reach either writer. This change adds the surface and nothing else; no repository, DAO, table or server code changed.
- **"Edit" was the one genuinely ambiguous word, so it was put to the user before any code.** A `delete_requests` row carries no free text: its editable content is the `entity_snapshot` of the target and the `target_base_version` it was observed at. The user chose **re-take the snapshot** (over "refresh only when the record actually changed" and over "withdraw and re-raise a fresh request"), and chose the **row-tap action sheet** as the surface (over inline buttons and a trailing overflow menu) — both the smaller and the more conventional option, matching Case History's action sheet and Manage Associates' member sheet.
- **Edit re-reads the record through its own feature's repository, not off a screen.** `DeleteRequestsViewModel.refreshSnapshot(id)` loads the request, refuses anything that is not still `pending`, resolves the target by `entity_type`/`entity_id` through `caseRepositoryProvider` / `caseHistoryRepositoryProvider` / `notesRepositoryProvider` / `referenceRepositoryProvider` (the same rows the original snapshot was taken from, and the same four kinds a request can name), and then re-uses the existing `deleteRequestSnapshot` / `deletionBaseVersion` helpers so the refreshed payload and version rule cannot drift from the ones the request was first raised with. A target this device no longer holds — deleted, or authored elsewhere and never pulled — is a **null the caller must handle**, and the screen says so rather than reporting a refresh that never happened. `cancel(id)` is the same shape around the existing `LocalDeleteRequestsRepository.cancel`, so the soft-delete and its queued `delete` were already correct and untouched.
- **One row, two states, one sheet.** In `delete_requests_screen.dart`, a **pending** row of `myDeleteRequestsProvider` now opens a bottom sheet with **Edit Request** and a red **Withdraw Request**; a **decided** row stays a plain, unkeyed container showing its status pill. The row widget gained `showStatusChip` rather than deriving the trailing affordance from `onTap`, because the requester's list carries both pending and decided rows — losing the pill on a pending row would have made it unreadable — while the reviewer's queue, where everything is pending by definition, keeps rendering exactly as before. Withdrawal is confirmed first (an edit can be repeated, a withdrawal cannot) and the confirmation says out loud that the record is *not* deleted and the request leaves the reviewers' queue. Both actions catch a refused or failed write and report "nothing changed", which is the honest wording for a request that was decided on another device.
- **Eight new ARB keys, appended in one block to `@@_section_deleteRequests`** in `app_en.arb` and `app_bn.arb`, key-for-key and order-for-order (995 keys each), Title Case in English, Bengali hand-written, `flutter gen-l10n` re-run. Both files' CRLF endings were preserved by the editor.
- **Tests:** 4 new in `test/features/delete_request_review_test.dart` — a pending row offers both actions, a decided row is not even a gesture target, editing re-takes the snapshot (the note's new title and `version` 7 land in the stored row while `status` stays `pending`, with exactly one queued `update` whose `changed_fields` name `entity_snapshot`/`target_base_version`/`updated_at`), and withdrawing writes nothing until the confirmation is tapped, then soft-deletes the row and queues exactly one `delete` carrying `is_deleted: true`. Full `flutter test` **1197 passed** (1193 → 1197); `flutter analyze` clean; ledger `--check` clean (no SQL this phase); `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.331`.
- **Resume point:** the requester side is now complete, so Task 20's remaining work is the task file's **acceptance checklist run item by item** (including the Rev 2.3/2.4 Alignment Addendum), plus the user's outstanding rulings on the previously flagged interpretive choices: the pending-deletion marker sits on the queue row rather than the record; the reviewer sees the requester's name best-effort and `deleteRequests_unknownRequester` otherwise; a queued-but-unsent review is not separately marked; and the Settings dashboard entry departs from the mockup-bound Settings body (recorded, task requirement wins).

### 2026-10-01 — Task 20 audit closure: a removed member can no longer merge a queued write — COMPLETE

- **The audit found one real server-side gap, and it was deeper than it first looked.** The Task 20 audit flagged that Phase 2 item 5 ("reject and never merge that member's queued mutations") was only partly enforced: `sync_can_write`'s author clause, `user_id = auth.uid()`, was checked before any firm helper, so a removed member could still push an **update** to a firm record they happened to author — and the phase note's claim that inserts/deletes were already refused was itself unverified. `20260930210000_sync_can_write_firm_membership.sql` replaces `sync_can_write` (the only write path a plpgsql body has) to close it.
- **The second defect is why the first went unnoticed — a SQL NULL trap.** The firm comparison was written `firm_id = current_firm_id()`. Once removal makes `current_firm_id()` NULL, that comparison is NULL, and `if not (<NULL>) then` does **not** branch in PL/pgSQL (`NOT NULL` is NULL, and a NULL condition is treated as "do not branch"). So the guard was permissive in exactly the case it existed for — a caller with no firm writing a row that has one — on **both** the INSERT and UPDATE paths. The fix replaces `=` with `is not distinct from` for every firm comparison, so a NULL on either side is a definite "no" rather than a slipped-through NULL; the `firm_id is null` disjunct still lets a personal row through.
- **Authorship no longer outlives membership, in all three places that trusted it.** A firm-scoped row may be written by its author only while that firm is still their firm (`firm_id is not distinct from current_firm_id()`); the RLS rule that an author may edit their own row (even without `can_edit`) is kept for current members. The same narrowing reaches a custom **reference** in a firm and a requester's own **pending delete request**, both of which were writable on `user_id` / `requested_by` alone. `can_edit_firm` / `can_delete_firm` still cover teammates and reviewers, and a `firm_id`-less personal row is untouched, so a removed member keeps their own diary. Only `sync_can_write` changed; `push_changes` already calls it.
- **Proven live, not just read.** `supabase/tests/push_changes_smoke_test.sql` gained a self-contained, rolled-back section: an associate (given their own premium entitlement so the refusal can never be the entitlement gate) authors a case and raises a request in the firm, proves the author rule still lets a **current** member edit their own row, is removed by the owner, and then has a queued **update**, a fresh **insert**, and a request **edit** all refused `permission_denied` with the rows provably unchanged — while the firm's owner can still edit the record. The first run of the smoke test exposed both defects (the NULL trap first, then the missing `is not distinct from`), which is exactly what a live check is for. The migration was applied to `lsthasywrdrhuuxnykau` (26 versions recorded) and refreshed directly with psql so the recorded version and the applied body agree; the ledger was regenerated (26 migrations, 575 KB) with its `0.0.330` group heading.
- **An offline guard so the live test is not the only witness.** `test/supabase/schema_contract_test.dart` gained a check that reads the new migration file directly (the `functionBlock` helper matches the first definition, which is the old file) and asserts every firm comparison uses `is not distinct from` and that none uses a plain `= public.current_firm_id()`. Both hard-coded migration counts moved 25 → 26 (`migration_applier_test.dart`, `all_supabase_sql_test.dart`), with `versions.last` now `20260930210000`.
- **Tests:** full `flutter test` **1193 passed** (1192 → 1193, the new contract test); `test/supabase/` 77 passed; `flutter analyze` clean; ledger `--check` clean; `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean; the live smoke test prints `SMOKE OK`. Version `0.0.330`.
- **Flagged, unchanged from the audit.** Phase 3 item 3 (the requester can edit/cancel their own pending request) is still **data-layer only** — `refreshSnapshot` / `cancel` / `watchMine` work and are now server-safe, but the requester's list rows are non-interactive, so there is no UI affordance yet. That one remains a decision for the user, as does the mockup departure for the Settings entry.
- **Resume point:** Task 20's remaining work is the user's rulings on the flagged interpretive choices (the requester edit/cancel UI; the pending-deletion marker's placement; the reviewer's best-effort requester name; the unmarked provisional review; the mockup departure), plus the task-file acceptance checklist run item by item.

### 2026-10-01 — Task 20: the requester's half of the delete-request surface reads its own scoped query — COMPLETE

- **Placement decided, and it stays one surface.** The badge entry and the requester's *My Delete Requests* list both live behind the single firm-only **Delete Requests** entry in **Settings → General**, in the same screen that carries the reviewer's queue. The reason is reachability rather than taste: the task's optional second placement is Manage Associates, and that entry is owner-only (`canOpenManageAssociatesProvider`), so a **requester** — by definition an associate or viewer without delete permission — could never reach their own list through it. The dashboard is the one entry every firm member can open, and a reviewer and a requester never coexist (somebody who may delete deletes directly and never raises a request), so one entry with two voices is both the smallest and the only correct surface. The already-flagged departure from the mockup-bound Settings body (AGENTS rule 9) is unchanged, and now also the recorded home of the requester list.
- **The requester side moved off the firm-wide stream onto `watchMine`.** `myDeleteRequestsStreamProvider` is new: a `StreamProvider` over `DeleteRequestsRepository.watchMine(accountCacheUserIdProvider)`, and `myDeleteRequestsProvider` is now a plain value alias over it. The requester's list **and** their badge (`deleteRequestsUnseenProvider`, the unread-decision count that `deleteRequestsBadgeCountProvider` returns for a non-deleter) both descend from that one scoped query, so a member's own request is never even fetched from the firm-wide stream, let alone counted out of it — it is the same scope the server grants that account. `deleteRequestsUnseenProvider` lost its redundant `requestedBy` filter with the move; only the *decided* and *unread* halves remain.
- **The reviewer side deliberately did not move.** The queue, the Settings badge for a `can_delete` member and the review screen need the whole firm's requests, not one author's, so they stay on `deleteRequestsStreamProvider` (`watchAll`); `watchMine` cannot answer "who is waiting on me". The screen's doc comment now states the split (two streams, on the one thing that decides what a person may see) rather than the old "one stream, sliced" claim, which was true before this change and is not true now.
- **Tests:** no new tests — the existing `delete_request_review_test.dart` covers the move, with `primePermissions` now also awaiting the new stream and the requester case asserting the list is entirely this account's own rows (not merely filtered to them). Targeted suites 43 passed; full suite **1192 passed**; `flutter analyze` clean; ledger `--check` clean (no SQL); `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.329`.
- **Resume point:** unchanged — Task 20's **acceptance checklist item by item** (including the Rev 2.3/2.4 Alignment Addendum), plus the user's calls on the flagged interpretive choices (the pending-deletion marker's placement on the queue row; the reviewer's best-effort requester name; the unmarked provisional review; the mockup departure for the Settings entry).

### 2026-10-01 — Task 20 Phase 4 (reviewer UI): the decision now has a screen, a badge and a writer — COMPLETE

- **The writer was already the right shape; it needed two verbs.** `DeleteRequestsRepository` gained `approve(id, {reviewNote})` and `reject(id, {reviewNote})`, both through one private `_review` that (like `refreshSnapshot`/`cancel`) writes the local row first and then queues a single `update` on `entity_type = 'delete_request'`, `baseVersion` = the version the device holds, `preMutationPayload` = the row as it stood. The only thing the server cannot infer from a plain update is a decision, so the payload carries `status` (`'approved'`/`'rejected'`), `review_note` and `reviewed_by`. Only a `pending` or `stale_review_required` request can be decided; anything already resolved is left exactly as it is, because rewriting a decision is how an audit trail is corrupted (and pinned by a test).
- **Reconfirm is not a second code path.** The server parks a stale request as `stale_review_required` *and adopts* the version it just saw, so a reconfirm is simply another `approve` against the row the device now holds — the screen words the button differently and nothing below it changes. The review screen reads the request back out of the live stream rather than holding the row it was opened with, so after a refused approval the parked, refreshed row is what the reviewer is looking at.
- **Two screens, one question each.** `delete_requests_screen.dart` is the reviewer's queue or the requester's own list — mutually exclusive in practice, since somebody who may delete never raises a request — and `delete_request_review_screen.dart` shows the entity's `entity_snapshot` (case number / note title / reference page / history step, plus the fields that entity is known by), the requester and the date, an optional review note, and Approve/Reject with a confirmation. A stale request puts the warning above the details and labels the action **Reconfirm**. Both sit on the established Settings sub-page shell and the shared sub-menu size contract; both are shortcuts-free in the sense that every read is the local database and every write is queued, so reviews stage offline exactly as the task requires.
- **The badge is per-account, and the requester gets one too.** The Settings dashboard gained a firm-only *Delete Requests* entry: a member with delete permission is badged with the queue in front of them, and a member without it with the decisions they have not read yet (the requester's half of item 4). "Read" is the one piece of state that is not a row, so it is a per-user preference — `delete_requests_seen_<userId>` in `SharedPreferences` — written when the screen opens; the stream providers for the queue, the requester's list and the badge are all sliced from the one local watch, so they can never disagree.
- **Flagged interpretive choices.** (1) The badge entry sits in Settings → General and is shown only in firm mode; the task's optional Manage Associates placement was not taken, to keep the change to one surface. (2) The reviewer is shown the requester's **name when the device already holds that profile** and a neutral *"A Firm Member"* otherwise, because the alternative is an online roster fetch and a review has to be stageable offline. (3) A provisional review (queued but not yet pushed) is not separately marked in the UI; it shows its new status optimistically like every other local write, and Sync Status still shows the mutation. Say the word on any of the three.
- **Also flagged: the Settings dashboard entry is a deliberate departure from the mockup.** AGENTS rule 9 binds screen bodies to `mockup_html_screens/`, and the Settings main screen has no mockup entry for a delete-request queue. Task 20 Phase 4 item 1 requires the badge in the Settings dashboard, so the task-specific requirement wins, and the deviation is recorded here rather than left silent.
- **Tests:** 8 new in `test/features/delete_request_review_test.dart` (queue vs requester visibility, the badge for each role, an individual's message, tapping a queue row into the review, approve writing the row and queueing exactly one `update` whose payload says `status = 'approved'`, a stale request reading as Reconfirm, reject keeping the record with its note, and the requester's list showing the decision while opening it clears the badge) plus 2 in `test/features/repositories_test.dart` (the writer's local row + queue entry; a decided request is not re-decided). The suite's fakes for `DeleteRequestsRepository` gained the two new methods. 1182 → **1192**.
- **Protocol:** `flutter analyze` clean; full `flutter test` **1192 passed**; ledger `--check` clean (no SQL: this phase is client-only, the server half landed in `0.0.327`); `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.328`.
- **Resume point:** Task 20 is now feature-complete on both sides. What remains is the task file's **acceptance checklist item by item** (including the Rev 2.3/2.4 Alignment Addendum), run top to bottom against 0.0.328, plus the user's calls on the three flagged choices above.

### 2026-09-30 — Task 20 Phase 4 (server side): the approval is a queued update the server decides, against a target it locks — COMPLETE

- **The decision is server-side, and the client sends only an intention.** An approval is an ordinary queued update on `entity_type = 'delete_request'` whose payload sets `status = 'approved'`; `20260930200000_push_changes_delete_review.sql` replaces `push_changes` whole (the only write path a plpgsql body has) to teach that update what it means. Everything happens in the mutation's existing savepoint, so the tombstone, the request's resolution and the receipt either all land or none do. `rejected` is deliberately **not** special-cased: it keeps the record, sets the status and notifies the requester through the update the requester-side code already knows how to make.
- **Locking, in one order.** The request row is already locked `for update` by the generic update path; the branch locks the target next and only then decides. Request-then-target is the *only* order used, so two concurrent approvals for different requests can never deadlock, and no decision is made against a row that moves underneath it. Authorization is the reviewer's `can_delete_firm(request.firm_id)`, checked explicitly because `push_changes` is `security definer` and RLS does not run inside it — and a request whose target belongs to a *different* firm is refused, since a delete request is firm-scoped and a cross-firm target can only be tampering.
- **An old snapshot never authorizes deletion.** The request records the version its author saw (`target_base_version`). If the target now sits at a different version the approval is refused as `target_conflict`, and the request is parked as `status = 'stale_review_required'` **and** adopts the version the reviewer just saw — so a reconfirm needs no new client contract at all: resubmitting the same approval now compares against the row that exists. That is what makes `stale_review_required` a real state rather than a message. A target that is missing or already soft-deleted becomes `already_resolved` with its tombstone.
- **Interpretive choice, flagged.** On `already_resolved` the request is left **exactly as it was** (still `pending`, no reviewer, version unchanged) because no review actually happened — recording `approved`/`reviewed_by` for a decision that never took effect would put a false entry in the audit trail. The alternative (auto-cancelling it) would need a rule the task does not state. Say the word if you would rather it resolve itself.
- **Atomic success, and the target rides back.** Only an unchanged target is tombstoned: `is_deleted`/version/`last_modified_by`/`updated_at` on the target, the same `case_history` cascade an ordinary case delete performs, and the request's resolution (`status = 'approved'`, `reviewed_by`, optional `review_note`, server time) — one savepoint. The result carries `target` + `target_entity_type` (plus `children` for a case), so the reviewer's device applies the record's disappearance in the same pass instead of waiting for a pull. Duplicate submissions of the same mutation id return the stored receipt verbatim, so a retry can never tombstone twice.
- **The client change is small and deliberate.** `push_worker.dart` gained `_applyReviewTarget` (a no-op for every non-review result, since both fields are null) and routes the new `target_conflict` status through the same path as `already_resolved`: the updated request row and the current target row are applied and the queue entry resolves. It is **not** treated as a data conflict — nothing is frozen and no `conflict_log` row is written, because the server accepted the submission and recorded the staleness; a person resolves it by reconfirming, not by picking a winner. The task's rule that a completed review never resolves *another* queue entry is honoured: the target's own queued mutations are left untouched.
- **A real bug the live test caught, not the compiler.** My first cascade guard compared the request's stored `entity_type` (`'case'`) against `'cases'`, so the cascade silently did nothing on an approved case delete — the tombstone landed and the children stayed live. The smoke test's cascade assertion failed on the live database and the guard now tests the resolved *table*. Noted because it is exactly the kind of wrong-but-plausible comparison that reading the SQL would not have exposed.
- **Tests:** the `push_changes_smoke_test.sql` gained a delete-review section (self-contained, runs live inside its rolled-back transaction) covering a self-approval refused with the request and target untouched, an edit moving the target under the request → `target_conflict` + `stale_review_required` with the current row returned, the reconfirm tombstoning atomically and resolving the request with reviewer/note, a duplicate submission returning the identical receipt without a second tombstone, a case target cascading to its `case_history` child, an already-deleted target → `already_resolved` with no decision recorded, and a plain rejection keeping the record. Offline: 2 new in `test/sync/push_worker_test.dart` (an approval applies the target tombstone; a stale review applies both rows, freezes nothing and writes no `conflict_log` row) and 1 new schema-contract test asserting the branch's shape in the ledger. 1179 → **1182**.
- **Live, not just applied.** The migration was applied to `lsthasywrdrhuuxnykau` (25 versions recorded) and — after the cascade-guard fix — refreshed directly with psql so the recorded version and the applied body agree; the smoke test then ran green against the live database. The ledger was regenerated (25 migrations, 567 KB, `--check` clean) with the new section and its `0.0.327` group heading, and both hard-coded migration counts moved 24 → 25.
- **Protocol:** `flutter analyze` clean; full `flutter test` **1182 passed**; `test/supabase/` 75 passed; ledger `--check` clean; `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.327`.

Two placements still need the user's sign-off (the badge: Settings dashboard only or also Manage Associates; the requester list: which Settings entry point, since the Settings main screen body is mockup-bound). Then the task's acceptance checklist, item by item.

### 2026-09-30 — Task 20 Phase 3: every delete asks one question, and a member who cannot delete raises a request instead — COMPLETE

- **One function decides, and nothing else does.** `delete_request_flow.dart`'s `deleteThroughFirmRules` is now the only place a case, a case-history entry, a note or a reference is deleted. It refreshes permissions first (the same `permissionFreshnessProvider` the other destructive confirmations call), then reads `canDeleteDirectlyProvider`: a member who may delete gets the ordinary window and the caller's own `deleteDirectly`; a member who may not gets a differently-worded window (*"Send A Deletion Request?"*, never *"Delete"*) and a queued `delete_requests` row plus the toast *"Deletion Request Sent To Admin/Owner."*. Wiring it here rather than in each screen is what stops the rule being applied on one screen and forgotten on the next; the four entity types are all covered (`case`, `case_history` in `case_detail_screen.dart`; `note` in `notes_screen.dart` and `note_detail_screen.dart`; `reference` in both the list's delete and the editor's save-then-delete path).
- **The request carries three real facts, none invented.** `createRequest` now takes a required `firmId` read from the cached profile — both the RLS insert policy and `sync_can_write` compare it to `current_firm_id()`, so a null there is a *refused* insert, not "no firm" — plus `entity_snapshot` (`deleteRequestSnapshot(row)`, the entity's own server-shaped row, which is what a reviewer reads and what proves the request meant *this* version) and `target_base_version` (`deletionBaseVersion(row.version)`, the version Phase 4 will compare against). It is queued through `sync_queue` under `entity_type = 'delete_request'` like any other push-domain row, which is what makes the workflow **offline-capable** (the task's item 2): nothing in the path touches the network.
- **The requester's own request can be re-snapshotted or withdrawn.** `refreshSnapshot` re-takes the snapshot and the observed version of a still-pending request (the record moved under it), and `cancel` withdraws it as a **soft** delete — `delete_requests` has no DELETE policy because the row is audit history. A request that was never pushed compacts away to nothing (insert + delete = a row the server never needed to know about); one the server has already seen becomes a tombstone. `watchMine(requestedBy)` scopes the list to its author for the UI Phase 4 will build.
- **A real defect found while making cancel-after-sync work, fixed for every push-domain table.** `SyncWriteHelper._record` read its prior mutations through `SyncQueueDao.getByEntity`, which returns **every** row the entity has ever had, `resolved` ones included — so a `resolved` insert counted as an open mutation. An entity whose first insert had already been pushed and resolved therefore swallowed every later change: a delete compacted to nothing at all (the server was never told), and an edit rewrote itself into a fresh *insert* the server would refuse as a duplicate id. The lookup now filters `state != 'resolved'`. This was not a delete-request bug — it applied to cases, notes and references too; it surfaced here because cancel-after-sync depends on the delete actually being queued. Pinned by two repository tests.
- **Honest scope note.** Phase 3's item 3 — "the requester can edit/cancel their own pending request" — is delivered at the **data layer** (`refreshSnapshot` / `cancel` / `watchMine`, RLS permits a requester's own pending update). There is deliberately **no requester-facing screen yet**: the approved plan places the requester list (`watchMine`) and the reviewer screen together in Phase 4, and the Settings main screen is mockup-bound (AGENTS.md rule 9), so a new entry point needs the user's sign-off on placement. Flagging rather than guessing.
- **Tests:** 10 new — 6 in `test/features/delete_request_flow_test.dart` (an individual and an owner each delete directly through the shared window; a member without delete permission raises a request carrying the real `firm_id`, snapshot and version and shows the sent toast; a member *with* the permission deletes directly; declining the request window changes nothing; and a firm member with no cached permissions fails closed and asks) and 4 in `test/features/repositories_test.dart` (the request is firm-scoped and snapshotted; re-snapshotting a pending request compacts into its queued insert; a never-pushed cancel compacts to nothing; a synced cancel is a tombstone; plus `watchMine` scoping). The flow suite drives the real permission providers off the cached rows and fakes only the repository and the refresh. 1169 → **1179**.
- **Protocol:** `flutter analyze` clean; full `flutter test` **1179 passed**; no SQL this phase, so the Supabase ledger and `AllSupabaseSql.md --check` are untouched; `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.326`. New ARB keys (5 per language) live in the appended `@@_section_deleteRequest` block, EN and BN in the same edit, `flutter gen-l10n` regenerated. (Two Flutter tool crashes from a stale `flutter_tester.exe` holding `build/native_assets/windows/sqlite3mc.dll` were cleared by terminating it; no source change.)
- **Resume point:** Task 20 Phase 4 — delete requests, reviewer side: replace `push_changes` in a new migration with a delete-review branch (`approved` locks request and target FOR UPDATE, requires the request still pending and the target at `target_base_version`; a changed target → `target_conflict` + `status = 'stale_review_required'`; an already soft-deleted target → `already_resolved`; success tombstones atomically with the `case_history` cascade, reviewer and server time, and idempotent receipts; `rejected` sets status/note only). Handle `target_conflict` in `push_worker.dart`. Then the reviewer screen, the pending badge in the Settings dashboard for `can_delete` members, and the requester's **My Delete Requests** list over `watchMine` — whose placement needs the user's confirmation. Remember a client cannot update `delete_requests` columns directly (`20260929102500` revoked table-level UPDATE), so an approval RPC is likely needed; and bump the two hard-coded migration counts (24 → 25) and add the new migration to `tool/generate_all_supabase_sql.dart`.

### 2026-09-30 — Task 20 Phase 2: removing an associate revokes access in one statement, and the removed device erases itself — COMPLETE

- **The revocation is a relink, not a session kill.** `remove_firm_member(p_user_id uuid)` (`20260930190000_remove_firm_member.sql`, `SECURITY DEFINER`, owner-only, resolved from `firms.owner_id` rather than a parameter) deletes the `firm_members` row and sets the member's `profiles.firm_id = null`, `role = 'individual'`. That one update **is** the revocation, because every guard in this schema reads `firm_id`: `current_firm_id()` scopes every RLS policy, `can_edit_firm()`/`can_delete_firm()` gate `sync_can_write()` — so a removed member's queued mutations are refused and never merged — and `can_read_topic()` authorizes the `firm:{id}` Realtime topic. The auth session itself is left alone on purpose: this project has no service-role key, the task's item 5 says so, and the guard chain makes it unnecessary. Authored records are untouched — `user_id` and `firm_id` both stay — which is the Addendum's "preserve immutable audit attribution without reassignment", and nothing is ever reassigned to the owner.
- **The live run corrected one of my own assumptions, and the tests now say the true thing.** The first end-to-end check asserted that a removed member reads none of the firm's rows and **failed**: `cases_select_scoped` is `user_id = auth.uid() or firm_id = current_firm_id()`, so a removed member still sees the rows they authored — which is exactly what preserving attribution means — and loses the firm's *other* rows. The committed section now asserts both halves (their own row still visible, the owner's row not visible), plus `can_read_topic('firm:…')` false, `not_found` on a second removal, `cannot_remove_self`, a non-owner refused with the membership intact, and `anon` denied EXECUTE.
- **A DELETE fires no row trigger, so the announcement is explicit.** The RPC sends two Broadcast pings itself — `user:{removed}` (their own topic is always readable, including after the relink, so their devices learn at once) and `firm:{firm}` (every remaining member's roster) — both as the trigger's own `row_change` event, so the client needs no second decoder. The same migration attaches the *existing* `broadcast_row_change()` to `firm_members`, which covers the other half of the phase: a permission toggle now reaches the member it affects.
- **On the client a membership ping is routed, never pulled.** `firm_members` is a pull-only cache and not one of the twelve synced tables, so `syncEntityForTable` returns null for it and the ordinary path would drop it; `RealtimeListener` therefore gained `onMembershipChange`, fired **at most once per window** however many frames arrived, and `sync_providers.dart` points it at the account refresh. That refresh is also the removal detector, and the evidence is deliberately durable rather than in-memory: `profiles.firm_id == null` (the server's current answer) **while a cached `firm_members` row still exists** (what this device last knew) can only mean one thing, it survives a restart, and it is invisible to an individual, to a live firm member, and to a device whose caches have already been cleared. `MembershipRevokedScope` latches on that and replaces the app; one action runs Task 19's **own** `wipeAndComplete()` (the complete children-first wipe, `sync_queue` included — which is what "discard or roll back the pending local mutations" reduces to) and then signs out. **Nothing is erased until the user taps**: the erasure is the irreversible step, so it is announced first, and no safety copy is taken — the task asks for the queue to be discarded, and the authored records live on the server.
- **The notice that had no consumer finally has one.** `SyncService.notice` has carried `permissionsChanged` and `clockSkew` since Task 18 with nothing in `lib/` rendering either, so a provisional edit rolled back by a permission change was silent and a skewed clock mis-stamped everything quietly. `SyncNoticeBanner` reads them through a new `syncNoticeProvider` and is mounted in the same two places the conflict banner is; it is **dismissible** where the conflict banner is not, and that difference is the design — a conflict is frozen work that still needs a person, a notice explains something sync has already finished with. An empty notice renders nothing at all, so neither host has to ask.
- **Manage Associates grows the rest of the owner's toolset.** Outstanding unused invites are listed (named by their creation date — only the code's SHA-256 is stored, so the plaintext is genuinely unrecoverable) and can be revoked behind a confirmation, because the associate holding the code cannot be told what it was. Tapping a member opens a sheet with the role pair (`associate`/`viewer` — `owner` is not a choice, since ownership is `firms.owner_id` and not a row flag), the three permission flags, and a **Remove From Firm** action behind a confirmation that names the cost. The owner's own row opens nothing, deliberately: a flag on their `firm_members` row would not change what they may do. Every write invalidates the roster rather than caching it, so the screen stays a read of the firm's server state.
- **Permission freshness before a destructive action.** `permissionFreshnessProvider` runs the same single `accountRefreshProvider` entry point the launch/resume/reconnect hooks use, and is wired in front of the five destructive delete confirmations that exist today (case, case history, note from the list, note from detail, reference). It is a **no-op offline**, on purpose: blocking the action would take away the provisional offline workflow the plan requires, and `push_changes` is authoritative either way. It is also a no-op for an individual, whose own diary cannot change under them. Phase 3's single delete decision point will call this same helper.
- **A destructive provisional action is now visibly marked.** `isDestructiveQueueOperation()` names the one place that decides what counts as destructive, and Sync Status renders a red **Deletion Pending** pill beside the state pill for a queued delete. The state pill alone said `pending`, which is true of every queued rename too. *Flagged interpretive choice:* the marker is on the queue row rather than on the (already locally soft-deleted, therefore invisible) case or note — moving it onto the record itself would mean unfiltering every case-list query, which is a larger change than this phase should make unasked. Say the word if you want it there instead.
- **Tests:** 23 new — 5 in `test/sync/realtime_listener_test.dart` (a membership ping is routed and never handed over as a pull, a burst is one refresh, a membership ping and a row ping both travel, a pending membership ping dies with the channel, and a pull-only cache is still not membership), 7 in `test/features/manage_associates_test.dart` (the invite list and the confirmed revoke, the empty-invite state, the member sheet writing the role and the three flags with untouched flags preserved, the confirmed removal, a declined removal removing nobody, the owner's own row inert, and a failed write reported), 6 new in `test/features/membership_revocation_test.dart` (removed → blocked, acting → queue discarded + wiped + session ended + routed to Login, nothing erased before the tap, and an individual, a live member and a not-yet-pulled profile each left untouched) and 4 new in `test/features/sync_notice_banner_test.dart` (nothing raised renders nothing, each notice named, acknowledging clears it), plus 2 in `test/features/sync_status_screen_test.dart` (a queued delete carries the pill, an ordinary edit does not). 1146 → **1169**. `FakeFirmRepository` gained the three new writes, and the two Supabase count guards moved 23 → 24.
- **Live, not just applied.** The migration is applied on `lsthasywrdrhuuxnykau` (24 recorded), the ledger regenerated (24 migrations, `--check` clean, with the new section and its `0.0.325` group heading in `tool/generate_all_supabase_sql.dart`), and `supabase/tests/rpc_smoke_test.sql` gained the `remove_firm_member` section. **Honest limitation, unchanged from Phase 1:** that file needs two real accounts and the project holds one, so the same assertions were exercised live in a rolled-back transaction with its own auth users — `SMOKE OK: remove_firm_member removes, revokes and preserves as specified` — and that temporary script was deleted rather than committed.
- **Protocol:** `flutter analyze` clean; full `flutter test` **1169 passed**; `test/supabase/` green; ledger `--check` clean; `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.325`. New ARB keys (25 per language) live in three appended sections — `@@_section_manageAssociatesPermissions`, `@@_section_syncNotice`, `@@_section_membershipRevoked` — plus `syncStatus_pendingDeletion` beside its block's siblings; EN and BN in the same edit, `flutter gen-l10n` regenerated, `test/l10n/arb_keys_test.dart` green.
- **Resume point:** Task 20 Phase 3 — delete requests, associate side: `LocalDeleteRequestsRepository.createRequest` currently writes `firmId: null`, which the RLS insert policy (`firm_id = current_firm_id()`) would refuse, so it must stamp the real firm id along with `entity_snapshot` and `target_base_version`; add edit/cancel of the requester's own pending request and a `watchMine`; build `delete_request_flow.dart` as the single decision point (`canDeleteDirectlyProvider` true → delete as today, false → create the request and toast *"Deletion request sent to Admin/Owner."*) and wire it into `case_detail_screen.dart` (case and history), `case_viewmodel.dart`, `notes_screen.dart`, `note_detail_screen.dart`, `notes_viewmodel.dart`, `case_reference_screen.dart` and `reference_viewmodel.dart`, for the four entity types `case`, `case_history`, `note`, `reference`. It should call this phase's `permissionFreshnessProvider` at the top, which is where the freshness hook finally belongs.

### 2026-09-30 — Task 20 Phase 1: an individual becomes a firm through a premium-gated transition, and Manage Associates finally exists — COMPLETE

- **The transition was already built; the gate was not.** Task 17 Phase 5's `ensure_firm` already creates the firm, sets `role = 'owner'`, relinks `profiles.firm_id` and upserts the owner's `firm_members` row, so Phase 1 item 1 needed no second RPC — reusing it was the user's decision. What it lacked is Phase 1 item 2's rule, and it is added **at the creation moment only**: a free (derived `is_premium`, never `subscription_status`) caller is refused with `{status: premium_required}`, while a **lapsed owner keeps the firm they already have**, because plan §6 promises exactly that and locking them out of their own chamber would be the opposite of the gate's purpose. `20260930180000_ensure_firm_premium_gate.sql` also closes a real privilege hole found while writing the gate: an **associate** calling the RPC would have created a second firm and relinked their profile away from the firm scoping every row they authored — invisible to them from then on. A member of somebody else's firm now gets `{status: member_of_another_firm}` having written nothing. `FOUND` is captured into a variable right after the firm lookup on purpose: the guard's own `exists (…)` resets it.
- **Manage Associates is real, and its entry point is conditional in both directions.** The screen (the plan's `[Design-deferred]` file, so no mockup — layout approved by the user first, in the established Settings sub-page shell) shows the offline sentence when offline, a sign-in prompt when there is no account, the roster for an owner, a create-firm card for an individual, and **"Only The Firm Owner Can Manage Associates."** for anyone else — an associate reaching it by deep link is never shown a create-firm form the server would refuse. `Accounts & Profile` gained the third entry, rendered through the new `canOpenManageAssociatesProvider` (wider than `canManageFirmProvider` on purpose: an individual *must* reach it, since adding the first associate is what creates the firm). The roster is a **live read** (`firmMembersListProvider` → `firm_members` + same-firm `profiles` names, both RLS-scoped), not a second copy of the membership state: those stay pull-only caches of this account's own row (AGENTS.md rule 4).
- **The plaintext code lives for one sheet and nothing else.** Creating the firm (or tapping Add Associate) generates the invite and shows the code once — selectable, copyable, shareable through the existing `AppShareService` (whose clipboard fallback means a device with no share target still ends with the code copied). It is never cached, never written and never logged; `GeneratedInvite.toString()` was already built to omit it.
- **A real defect found by building the gate: entitlement was read once and never revisited.** `premiumAccessProvider` is a `FutureProvider` whose body read the database, so it kept the first answer it saw — on a cold start that is `false` before the profile pull lands, which is exactly how a **premium** account would have been sent to the Paywall (and, on the other side, how the free-limit gate could have stayed closed). It now **watches** the cached profile, so a pulled renewal or upgrade recomputes it, and `accountRefreshProvider` invalidates it after every successful refresh — the case that matters for a firm member, whose entitlement lives on the firm row the server mirrors and which can move without the profile moving. Watching `accountFirmProvider` there instead was tried first and **hung the provider** (Riverpod never resolved the future), so the firm case is covered at the refresh entry point instead. `resolvePremiumAccess(db, userId, now:)` is untouched, so the Task 07 acceptance tests still pin the DB-based rule.
- **Live, not just applied.** The migration was applied to `lsthasywrdrhuuxnykau` (the applier's own project-ref guard; 22 of 23 already recorded, 1 applied) and the ledger regenerated — 23 sections, `--check` clean, with the new section and its version-group heading added to `tool/generate_all_supabase_sql.dart`. `supabase/tests/rpc_smoke_test.sql` gained an `ensure_firm` section covering all five behaviours: a free caller refused and **no firm created**, a premium individual reaching `ready` with the profile relinked, the owner role set and every permission on their `firm_members` row, a second call returning the same firm, the member-of-another-firm guard refusing without relinking, and `anon` denied EXECUTE. **Honest limitation:** that file needs two real accounts and the project currently holds one, so the same assertions were exercised live in a rolled-back transaction whose own auth users it created (the `push_changes_smoke_test.sql` pattern) — `SMOKE OK` — and that temporary script was deleted rather than committed.
- **Tests:** 9 new in `test/features/manage_associates_test.dart` (offline body replaces the controls; a free account is sent to the Paywall and creates nothing; a premium individual's create-firm action calls `ensure_firm` then invites once and shows the code; the roster renders names and roles; the owner's Add Associate creates no second firm; a failed roster read is reported; an associate is never offered a create-firm form; and the menu entry is offered to an individual and hidden from an associate). `test/features/auth_test_support.dart`'s fake firm repository gained `listMembers`, and the two Supabase count guards (migration folder and ledger) moved 22 → 23 with their reasons extended. **Protocol:** `flutter analyze` clean; full `flutter test` **1146 passed**; `test/supabase/` 75 passed; ledger `--check` clean; `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.324`. New ARB keys live in two blocks — `settingsAccountProfile_manageAssociatesSubtitle` beside its block's siblings, `auth_errorPremiumRequired` in the auth block, and a new `@@_section_settingsManageAssociates` appended at the end — EN and BN in the same edit, `flutter gen-l10n` regenerated.
- **Resume point:** Task 20 Phase 2 — invites and permission management: the outstanding-invite list and revoke, the per-member role selector and Add/Edit/Delete toggles, `remove_firm_member` (owner-only, SECURITY DEFINER: delete the `firm_members` row, relink the removed member's `profiles.firm_id`/`role` to individual) with its membership-revocation broadcast, permission freshness before every online permission-sensitive action, the visible pending marker on destructive provisional actions, the app-wide notice banner that finally renders `SyncNotice.permissionsChanged` / `clockSkew`, and the `membershipRevoked` branch of Task 19's logout flow (discard the queue, then the existing wipe and sign-out).

### 2026-09-30 — The Task 19 audit's four gaps are closed: a copy you can actually reopen, no cache left behind, and a re-login that waits — COMPLETE

- **Gap 1 — the logout safety copy was written where nothing could restore it.** The branched logout exported through `LocalArchiveSink` into the app's **private** `<documents>/backups`, while the plan (§5, twice) requires the copy "in exactly the same manner and format as `Settings > Backup & Restore > Backup to Local Storage`" and promises *"…You can restore it after logging back in."* Backup to Local Storage writes through the **Save-to gateway**, and Restore reads **only** through the file picker — so on Android the logout copy sat in a directory the picker cannot reach, and the wipe even erased the recorded path. `logoutArchiveSinkProvider` now delegates to `backupFileGatewayProvider`, so the logout export is the identical path the manual action uses and the user chooses where it lands. The extended-offline copy (Phase 5) keeps its own `LocalArchiveSink` for the reason that choice exists — the app is locked and there is nobody to answer a Save-to window — which the sink's own doc already said. A dismissed Save-to window is now mapped to `LogoutResult.exportCancelled` (via `BackupSaveCancelledException`) rather than to a failure, so cancelling still deletes nothing and says so in the right words.
- **Gap 2 — the Addendum's "invalidates profile/preferences caches" was only half done.** `AuthViewModel.signOut()` invalidated the Riverpod profile provider and cleared the auth identity keys, and the Drift wipe emptied `user_preferences` — but the **SharedPreferences-backed** per-user values survived: the saved profile record (`ProfileStore`: full name, bar association, membership number, office address, mobile), the profile picture, the backup metadata/schedule/nudge stamps, and the chosen language. Given the "the account's values fill the device's blanks only" rule, a stale record could even have suppressed the **next** user's pulled values. Each store gained a `clear`/`reset` that removes its keys (`ProfileStore.clear`, `ProfileFieldsNotifier.clear`, `BackupMetadataNotifier.reset`, `LocaleNotifier.reset`; the picture notifier already had one), and `signOut()` calls them after `_local.clear()`, so every branched-logout and account-switch path leaves nothing of the previous holder behind.
- **Gap 3 — the forced re-login could strand one account's data behind the next sign-in.** `SessionExpiryNotice` awaited `runBranchedLogout` and then navigated to Login **unconditionally**; if the user declined the offline block or cancelled the passphrase, nothing was wiped yet the app still left for Login, leaving the previous account's diary in place across the switch — the cross-contamination §5 exists to prevent. `runBranchedLogout` now returns **true only when the session was actually ended**, and the notice navigates only on true; every cancellation returns false and changes nothing. Manage Account's `_signOut` uses the same result so a cancelled logout no longer pops the screen, matching its own "closes when it is done" comment.
- **Gap 4 — the two conflict queries disagreed about what was open.** `ConflictLogDao.getUnresolved()` filtered `is_dismissed = false` while `watchUnresolved()` — the one the banner and `unresolvedConflictsProvider` read — did not, and `dismiss()` has no production caller. Dismissal is UI-only and never resolves a conflict (plan §1), so a dismissed-but-unresolved conflict must stay visible; the filter was removed from `getUnresolved()` (dead code today, but the two can no longer disagree) and its doc now says why.
- **Not changed, deliberately.** The two *documented interpretive deviations* the audit raised are left standing because neither is a defect: the Realtime channel closes on `paused`/`hidden`/`detached` but not `inactive` (a call or the app switcher must not end the subscription, or the continuity throttle would be dead code), and Sync Status title-cases its data-derived entity/operation labels at render time rather than adding a dozen ARB keys (the values are queue columns, not UI copy). The Phase 5 write-guard ceiling stays as the `ponytail:` note it is.
- **Tests:** no new tests — the four fixes are all covered by the existing suites, which is why they were found by reading rather than by the suite. The logout flow/dialog tests still pass unchanged (they inject a recording sink, so the provider's destination swap is invisible to them), and `manage_account_test`'s cancel case still asserts the session and the rows survive. **Protocol:** `flutter analyze` clean; full `flutter test` **1137 passed**; `flutter gen-l10n` regenerated for the changed `logout_backedUpTo` copy (EN and BN); no SQL touched, so the Supabase ledger and its `--check` are untouched; `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.323`.
- **Resume point:** Task 19 and its audit repairs are complete. What remains of the task is its acceptance checklist run item by item (a report), plus the three device-only items reported honestly rather than claimed (a live two-client Realtime ping, the 150-day lock on a real clock, the three logout branches against real connectivity) — and now also verifying on a device that a logout copy really is reachable from Restore. One non-blocking item still stands: the Review Conflict and Sync Status screens' layouts were never formally signed off (built in the established Settings sub-page shell, as permitted).

### 2026-09-30 — Task 19 Phase 6: logging out now settles up before it erases, and never erases a copy it cannot replace — COMPLETE

- **One planner, three branches, and the branch is a decision, not a script.** `lib/features/auth/data/logout_flow.dart` holds `LogoutFlow`, whose `plan()` returns a `LogoutPlan` naming the branch (`syncedThenWiped`, `blockedOffline`, `freeExportThenWipe`, `cleanWipe`) and the count of unsynced rows, and whose `syncThenComplete()` / `wipeAndComplete()` / `exportThenComplete(passphrase)` carry out exactly one of them. `lib/features/auth/presentation/logout_dialogs.dart`'s `runBranchedLogout(context, ref, {cannotSync})` is the only caller the UI needs, so the account screen, the forced re-login notice and any future entry point obey the same rule instead of each re-deciding it. The plan is pure and pinned by test, so "which branch does this account get" is answered without a widget in sight.
- **The rule every branch serves: nothing that exists only on this device may be destroyed without a copy.** A free account **always** auto-exports first (plan letter (c)); a premium account that is offline with unsynced rows is blocked with *"Connect to the internet to sync your changes first."* and an explicit **"Log out anyway"**, whose warning names what logging out now discards before **Proceed** exports and wipes (letter (b)); a premium account that is online runs the mandatory sync and wipes only once it succeeds (letter (a)). A premium account with an empty queue takes the `cleanWipe` branch and asks nothing, because the server already holds every row and there is no unique copy to lose.
- **The wipe is Phase 5's, and it is complete.** `_wipeAndEnd()` calls `BackupRepository.wipeAllTables()` — the same children-first single transaction the recovery flow uses, **without** `keepMetadataKeys`, so the local Drift database and the whole `sync_queue` go together — and only then ends the session through `AuthViewModel.signOut()`, which already invalidates the profile cache (`profileRepositoryProvider.invalidate()`) and clears the per-user local state. The Addendum's "complete wipe scope and invalidates profile/preferences caches" is therefore met by composition rather than by a second wipe path. The export is Task 15's own container (`encryptBackup`, same versioned encrypted format and the same *"Your data has been backed up to …"* message) written through a new `LocalArchiveSink`; `logoutArchiveSinkProvider` is the seam the tests override, so production writes to the app's own backups directory while a test keeps the bytes in memory. **A cancelled or empty passphrase wipes nothing** — `encryptBackup` refuses an empty secret, and the flow treats it as `exportCancelled` — so the user can always back out.
- **The forced re-login path is the same branches, with one honest flag.** `lib/features/auth/presentation/session_state.dart`'s `SessionExpiryNotice` action now calls `runBranchedLogout(..., cannotSync: true, ...)` and then routes to the login screen, because a rejected session cannot push and must never claim it did. `cannotSync` skips the mandatory-sync branch and drops straight to the warning-then-export path, so the user is warned and given a copy rather than blocked forever on a `syncThenComplete()` that can never succeed.
- **Two decisions worth recording, both user-approved.** The 150-day recovery lock of Phase 5 is firm-mode-only (already recorded); here, the unsyncable premium+online case (a queue the sync cannot clear) **warns, exports, then allows the logout** — the same fallback an offline user gets — because a user who cannot ever log out is worse served than one who was warned and handed their data.
- **Tests:** 20 new — 12 in `test/features/logout_flow_test.dart` (the pure plan for every plan/connectivity/queue combination, and each executor's write-before-wipe ordering, the cancelled and failing export paths, and that `syncThenComplete` never wipes on a failed pass), 6 in `test/features/logout_dialogs_test.dart` (a clean wipe asks nothing; a free account backs up then wipes and names the file; cancelling the passphrase deletes nothing; the offline block asks twice in order *and in the right order* and then exports; declining it ends the logout untouched with no export; and a sync that cannot clear the queue warns then exports) and 2 new in `test/features/manage_account_test.dart` (cancelling the backup keeps both session and data; a synced account logs out at once, asking nothing), with the old direct-sign-out test rewritten to the free export-then-wipe branch — 1117 → **1137**. The dialog actions carry keys (and the test scopes its taps to the `AlertDialog`) because `logout_proceed` is also labelled "Log Out", the same words as the menu entry that opens it. **Protocol:** `flutter analyze` clean; full `flutter test` **1137 passed**; `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.322`. No SQL changed, so the Supabase ledger and its `--check` are untouched.
- **Resume point:** Task 19 is implemented in full across its six phases. What remains is the task file's own acceptance checklist, run item by item with the Addendum's items included — a report, not new code. Three items can only be shown on a device and are reported honestly rather than claimed: a live two-client Realtime ping, the 150-day lock on a real device clock, and the three logout branches against real connectivity. Non-blocking carry-over from the phase audits: the Review Conflict screen's layout was never formally signed off (it was built in the established Settings sub-page shell, as Sync Status was), and `ConflictLogDao.getUnresolved()` filters `is_dismissed = false` while `watchUnresolved()` does not — the banner therefore watches a superset, and `dismiss()` currently has no caller in `lib/`.

### 2026-09-30 — Task 19 Phase 5: a firm device that stayed away too long now rebuilds before it can write — COMPLETE

- **Firm mode only, per your instruction, and it is the right line.** The risk the lock exists for is *another writer* deleting a row while this device was away, so the device cannot tell a row it never saw from one the firm removed — and pushing would resurrect it (Sync Plan §3). An individual diary has one author, so nothing can have been deleted behind it, and locking a single-author device out of its own edits is pure harm. `shouldRecover` therefore requires firm mode, the whole flow — lock, safety copy, wipe — is skipped for an individual account, and **no server work happens for one either**. This is a deliberate narrowing of the plan's Phase 5 wording, which made no mode distinction in the task file and only a loose "premium/Firm mode" in §3. It is now recorded as a *Resolved Design Clarification* in `docs/implementation_plan.md` — **"Extended-Offline Recovery Lock Applies to Firm Mode Only (150 days, per-device)"** — with the plan's own §3 sentence and its summary bullet corrected in the same pass (so a later review cannot re-report the firm gate as a gap), a matching clarification note under the task file's Phase 5 heading, and the §3 `SharedPreferences` note corrected to the durable `sync_metadata` placement the Revision 2.4 Addendum requires.
