- **The check is per-device and is never the profile's number.** `lib/sync/recovery_lock.dart` compares **this device's** own `last_successful_sync_at` against a 150-day threshold (the plan's 30-day buffer under the server's 180-day tombstone retention). `profiles.last_successful_sync_at` is deliberately not read: another device's recent sync would hide exactly the gap being looked for. A device that has never synced is not locked — a fresh install is what the initial pull is for. `lib/sync/recovery_flow.dart` runs the check when the cached profile arrives and when connectivity returns, and it catches its own failures so a check that cannot run never breaks a launch.
- **The lock is committed before anything else, and it survives being killed.** `RecoveryLock.engage` writes the flag **and** the checkpoint in one `sync_metadata` transaction, before any export or network work, and both are read back from the database rather than held in memory — so a device killed mid-recovery comes back locked. Proceed re-stamps the checkpoint inside the same transaction as the wipe. `SyncService._isEntitled()` refuses every run while the lock is set, which is what pauses both workers on the ordinary paths.
- **The blocking screen is the whole app.** `RecoveryRequiredScope` renders the app untouched when the lock is off and replaces it entirely when it is on, so there is no screen to edit, no FAB to press and no route to reach one — the plan's "editing/FAB disabled with a recovery-required message", done completely. It is an overlay rather than a route because it must sit above every screen including the one the app opens on, and must return on the next launch without navigation. Its passphrase fields are inline rather than the Backup screen's shared dialog, because this widget sits above the app's navigator and has none to show a dialog on; the labels are the shared ones.
- **Everything is exported before anything is erased, and the copy is verified before the wipe.** `BackupRepository.exportSnapshot` is the Task 15 path, so the safety copy is the same versioned encrypted container every other export writes — and its `backupTableOrder` already covers all 21 tables, `sync_queue` (the pending, blocked and conflicted rows) and `sync_metadata` included. The only difference is the destination: a new `LocalArchiveSink` writes into the app's own `documents/backups` directory instead of through a Save-to dialog, because the app is locked and nobody is there to answer one. **Proceed** re-reads the file, compares its checksum, and refuses to erase anything if it does not match — the passphrase is never generated or stored, since an archive nobody can open would be worse than none.
- **The wipe is one transaction, and only a completed rebuild clears the lock.** Every table is emptied children-first in a single durable transaction; the only thing preserved is the safety copy's own receipt (`archive.path`/`checksum`/`state`), because it describes a file outside the database and is what Proceed verifies against on a retry. Then the seed ledger is re-run, then a full authenticated pull from the sentinel, and only then is the flag cleared — so any failure anywhere leaves the lock set and the flow simply starts again. No session material is held in the database at all (the signed-in session lives in platform storage, which is never touched), so nothing has to be carried across and no reauthentication step is needed.
- **One honest limit, recorded rather than hidden.** The write guard lives in `SyncWriteHelper._record` — the single place every queued mutation passes through — which is *after* a repository's own Drift write. A repository reached through some non-UI path therefore leaves an unsynced local row behind when the guard throws. It is harmless in this flow (recovery erases every table, so nothing survives it), and it is marked with a `ponytail:` comment naming the ceiling and the upgrade path (hoist the guard to the repository entry points) rather than pretending the rejection is stricter than it is.
- **Tests:** 16 new — 13 in `test/sync/recovery_lock_test.dart` (five on the pure rule: past the threshold recovers, inside it does not, exactly 150 days is enough, an individual account never does however long it was away, and a device that has never synced does not; the flag and the checkpoint read back through a **fresh** `RecoveryLock` over the same database, which is the durability claim; individual mode and a recent sync both leave the flag unset; a firm device past the threshold locks and a second check is idempotent; the safety copy is recorded and its bytes are the container rather than readable JSON; no copy means Proceed refuses; a checksum mismatch stops the wipe with the local row still present; offline keeps the lock and erases nothing; and a verified copy actually wipes, walks all twelve tables, re-seeds the reference data, clears the lock and **keeps the archive receipt**) and 3 in `test/features/recovery_required_screen_test.dart` (unlocked passes through; locked blocks the app and disables Proceed until a copy exists; a recorded copy names the file, enables Proceed, and Proceed asks first and cancelling changes nothing). **Protocol:** `flutter analyze` clean; full `flutter test` **1117 passed**; `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.320`. No SQL changed, so the Supabase ledger is untouched.
- **Resume point:** Task 19 Phase 6 — the branched logout: (a) premium + online runs a mandatory sync before the wipe; (b) premium + offline blocks with "Connect to the internet to sync your changes first." plus an explicit "Log out anyway" leading to the warning and Proceed/Close, where Proceed always auto-exports first; (c) a free account always auto-exports then wipes. It reuses this phase's wipe and `LocalArchiveSink`, routes the forced re-login path (Task 17 Phase 4) through the same branches, and follows the same complete wipe scope and preference/profile-cache invalidation. Then the task's acceptance checklist including the Addendum.

### 2026-09-30 — Task 19 Phase 4: the queue can now be seen, and a stuck change can be answered — COMPLETE

- **One list, four views, and nothing hidden.** `lib/features/sync/presentation/sync_status_screen.dart` is the plan's Sync Status screen, built in the established Settings sub-page shell because no mockup HTML exists for it (the task file says to follow the Justice Ledger system and confirm the layout; the layout was confirmed with the user before it was built: header badge **and** Online Sync as the two entry points, inline actions with a `⋯` overflow, one whole-queue export in the app bar). The screen reads a single stream — `SyncQueueDao.watchOpen()`, which excludes only `resolved` — and derives its chips and their counts from it, so **All can never disagree with the badge that opened the screen**, and no `pending`, `pushing`, `conflict_pending` or `blocked` row can be filtered out of existence. Each card carries the six things the plan names: entity, operation, state, retry count, last error and the persisted `last_attempt_at` (the Addendum's item), plus the created time.
- **Retry and Discard, and Discard's four honest endings.** Retry returns a `blocked` entry to `pending` with a clean retry count and no error — a state change, not a push, so it cannot bypass the coalescing window or the entitlement gate; the ordinary pending-count trigger sends it. Discard drops the mutation first (so nothing retries it mid-rollback) and then puts the record back to a state the server agrees with: it asks the server for the authoritative row and applies it (`rolledBackFromServer`); if the server has no such row, the local copy was provisional and is removed (`removed`, via a new `SyncTableBinding.deleteLocal`); offline it restores the pre-mutation snapshot the permission-rejection path already uses (`rolledBackLocally`); and when even that is absent it drops the mutation and invents nothing (`dropped`). That is the plan's "authoritative-row rollback/pull" with no destructive guessing, and all four endings are pinned by test.
- **The bundle is metadata-only unless someone says otherwise, and it is encrypted either way.** `lib/core/backup/diagnostic_bundle.dart` builds the structure and seals it with Task 15's `encryptBackup`; a metadata-only bundle **omits** the payload columns rather than blanking them, so it holds no case content at all, and including payloads needs a separate consent dialog after the scope sheet. The bundle records `includes_payloads`, its own `expires_at` (seven days) and the sync metadata a support reader asks about first (`last_successful_sync_at`, `clock_offset_ms`); the sheet shows the deletion reminder before anything is written. The file is `.lcdiag`, deliberately not `.lcbak`, so the restore picker can never offer it as a backup.
- **Two ways in, and a badge that means something.** `AppRoutes.settingsSyncStatus` is its own route (the approved layout), reached from a header badge that appears **only** when a `blocked` or `conflict_pending` entry exists — ordinary pending work is not news — and from a card on Online Sync. `Review Conflict` on a frozen row opens the Phase 3 screen for the newest unresolved conflict on that entity; with none it says so rather than opening a blank page.
- **Tests:** 17 new — 5 in `test/core/diagnostic_bundle_test.dart` (a metadata-only bundle carries no payload key at all; payloads appear only on consent and are decoded for the reader; counts and the self-declared expiry; the file-name stamp; and a real round trip — the written bytes are the container, `decryptBackup` reopens exactly the bundle, and an empty secret is refused), 7 in `test/sync/sync_status_test.dart` (each filter matches only its states; counts agree with the filters and the badge excludes pending; and Discard's four endings over a real database and a fake transport, plus Retry), 5 in `test/features/sync_status_screen_test.dart` (the empty state; All listing both rows with the chips filtering them; Retry returning a blocked row to the queue; Discard confirming first and then rolling the record back; and the export sheet gating payloads behind an explicit consent). **Protocol:** `flutter analyze` clean; full `flutter test` **1101 passed**; `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.319`. No SQL changed, so the Supabase ledger is untouched this phase.
- **Resume point:** Task 19 Phase 5 — extended-offline recovery (150 days, per-device, reading the device-local `last_successful_sync_at` and **never** `profiles.last_successful_sync_at`): the durable `extended_offline_recovery_required` flag set before any export or network work and surviving process death, the lock enforced at `SyncWriteHelper._record` and at database open, the automatic encrypted safety export under the lock, and the blocking screen whose **Proceed** verifies the checksum, wipes every app-state class in one transaction while preserving only the minimum session material, reconstructs from a full authenticated pull and clears the lock (any failure keeps it), and whose **Close** leaves the app locked without cloud writes. Then Phase 6's branched logout, then the task's acceptance checklist including the Addendum.

### 2026-09-30 — Task 19 Phase 3: a conflict now has exactly one answer path, and the answer travels — COMPLETE

- **One resolver, so the push and the pull cannot disagree.** Detection already happened in two places — the push worker when `push_changes` refuses a mutation, the delta pull when an authoritative row intersects queued work — and the *decision* now happens in one: `lib/sync/conflict_resolution.dart` (`ConflictResolver.reconcile/resolveWithFields/rebaseOntoNewRecord/discard`). Both workers call it and neither keeps its own copy of the conflict logic (`push_worker._logConflict`/`_encode` are gone, `pull_worker`'s inline log write is gone). The verdict itself is pure and testable without a database: `lib/sync/conflict_rebase.dart`'s `planConflictRebase(base, server, local, changedFields)`. It compares against the mutation's **own** `changed_fields` when present and falls back to every differing field for an older row, ignores the bookkeeping columns every write touches (`id`, `version`, `updated_at`, `synced_at`, `server_updated_at`, `last_modified_by`, `created_at`, and `is_deleted`, which is answered by the tombstone verdict before any field comparison), and reports one of three verdicts.
- **Disjoint edits rebase themselves; nothing else does.** A disjoint change is laid over the authoritative row and re-sent as a **new** mutation — fresh `mutation_id` (the rejected one already has a server receipt, so replaying it would return the same rejection), `base_version` = the server's, the server row as both the new base and the rollback snapshot — and the entity never freezes. The conflict is still written to `conflict_log`, but already answered (`resolution_state = 'rebased'`, `resolved_at` set, `mergeable_conflict = true`), so it is an audit trail rather than a to-do. Overlap — the same field changed to two values — and **coupling** stay for a person: `syncFieldGroups` names the three field sets that are one logical value (the next hearing step's `next_date`/`last_step`/`notes_results`, the case identity, the filing details), so editing any one of a group is editing the group. Those, and **every tombstone**, freeze the entity (`conflict_pending`) and preserve every later queue entry.
- **The user's answers are three, and all three are explicit.** The Review Conflict screen (`lib/features/sync/presentation/review_conflict_screen.dart`) shows the server value and the attempted value for each colliding field, the editor's name resolved from the same-firm `profiles` cache via the server row's `last_modified_by` (never invented — the fallback is "Another Device"), and the later queue entries the freeze is preserving. `resolveWithFields` sends one new mutation against the server's version; `rebaseOntoNewRecord` copies the local work onto a **new** id (server-owned columns cleared, timestamps reset, queued as an insert) so a deleted row is never resurrected; `discard` rolls the authoritative row back into place behind a confirmation. Only `ConflictLogDao.setResolution` writes `resolution_state`, so a **dismissal or elapsed time can never look like a resolution** — pinned by test.
- **The banner is app-wide and non-dismissible.** `SyncConflictBanner` watches `ConflictLogDao.watchUnresolved()` and renders nothing at all when the list is empty. It sits **above the bottom navigation bar** inside `CasesScreenModal` (every case-list screen), and `AppShellRoot` renders it for the shell screens that have no nav bar — the two are mutually exclusive by route, so no screen shows it twice and none shows none. Tapping it pushes the Review screen, so Back returns where the user was.
- **`mergeable_conflict` from day one, local and mirrored.** Drift `schemaVersion` 9 → 10 with `if (from < 10) m.addColumn(...)`; `supabase/migrations/20260930160000_conflict_mergeable_column.sql` adds the column (`not null default false`, the safe direction) plus its comment; the mirror payload carries it on both the insert and every later resolution update, so a conflict raised on one device reads as answered on another instead of being recomputed there. The **90-day tombstone purge guard** the task also asks for was already implemented (`purged only when synced_at IS NOT NULL AND synced_at >= updated_at`); it now has a regression test instead of only a comment.
- **A real server defect, found by adding the mirror to the smoke test.** `push_changes` strips the client's `created_at` for `conflict_log` (the column is a server `timestamptz` defaulted to `now()`) and then put the client's epoch milliseconds straight back whenever the table had a `created_at` column at all — `jsonb_populate_record` cast `1790757617282` into a `timestamptz`, raising `22007`, which the per-mutation handler turns into a **permanent rejection**. So **no conflict mirror could ever land**: a conflict raised on one device was never reviewable on another. Caught by adding a `conflict_log` row to `supabase/tests/push_changes_smoke_test.sql` and running it, not by reading the function. `supabase/migrations/20260930170000_push_changes_created_at_guard.sql` replaces the function with one corrected condition — the fallback now also requires the column's **type** to be an epoch-ms integer — and both migrations are applied live on `lsthasywrdrhuuxnykau`, recorded in `supabase_migrations`, with the ledger regenerated (22 migrations). The smoke test then passes end to end: the insert lands with `mergeable_conflict = true`, and a later update by `id` lands `user_resolved`.
- **Tests:** 40 new — 8 in `test/sync/conflict_rebase_test.dart` (both sides kept on disjoint fields; the same field twice differing needs a person; the same value twice does not; two fields of one step are coupled; a tombstone is never merged whatever the fields say; bookkeeping columns never make a conflict; an empty change list falls back to every differing field; an absent value and a null are the same value), 8 in `test/sync/conflict_resolution_test.dart` (the three reconcile verdicts, `resolveWithFields`, `rebaseOntoNewRecord`, `discard`, and that a dismissal never resolves), 5 in `test/database/tombstone_purge_test.dart` (purged only when synced and older than 90 days; an unsynced tombstone survives 400 days; a tombstone edited after its last sync survives; a recent one is kept; live rows are never touched), 5 in `test/features/sync_conflict_review_test.dart` (no conflict means no banner; one conflict shows the count and opens the screen with both candidate values; an answered conflict never reaches the banner; Save writes one replacement mutation, clears the banner and marks `user_resolved`; a tombstone offers only the two explicit answers and the copy lands as a new insert), plus the rewritten push/pull worker conflict tests (an overlap freezes; a **disjoint** conflict rebases itself and needs no person). **Protocol:** `flutter analyze` clean; full `flutter test` **1084 passed**; `test/supabase/` 75 passed; `push_changes_smoke_test.sql` `SMOKE OK` against the live project; ledger `--check` clean; `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.318`.
- **Resume point:** Task 19 Phase 4 — the Sync Status / Diagnostics screen: filter chips (All / Pending / Conflicted / Blocked) over the queue list, per row the entity, operation, state, retry count, last error and the persisted `last_attempt_at`; per-row Retry, Discard (confirmation plus authoritative rollback) and Review Conflict; an Export Diagnostic Bundle that is metadata-only by default and encrypted when payloads are included; a blocked/conflicted badge in the header; and the entry point from Online Sync. **No mockup HTML exists for that screen**, so its layout follows the Justice Ledger design system and is confirmed with the user before it is built — the same gap applies to the Review Conflict screen built here, which was laid out in the established Settings sub-page shell and should be signed off when the Phase 4 layout is.

### 2026-09-30 — Task 19 Phase 2: a dropped socket is now retried on a ladder, and a resume pull is skipped only when continuity is proven — COMPLETE

- **The ladder, and where it stops.** `lib/sync/sync_health.dart` owns the reconnect schedule (1s → 2s → 4s → 8s → 16s → 32s, 63 s total, plan §1) and `SyncHealthController` drives it off the listener's status rather than off the socket, so an automatic re-join and a refused join are treated the same way. Past the last rung active retrying stops **on purpose**: retrying then would be polling, so the `connectivity_plus` stream, the next resume, or the user's tap is what restarts it. The ladder also stops while the app is backgrounded or the link is down (`setForeground`/`setOnline`), because retrying into a dead socket is exactly the waste §12.3 forbids.
- **The paused indicator is real, and tapping it does something.** After three consecutive failures — a push, a pull or a lost channel all count — the header's existing sync button shows the `SyncState.paused` state and its "Sync paused" tooltip (both already in the ARB). Tapping calls `SyncService.retryNow()`, which clears the service's own backoff window **and** the channel's ladder, rejoins, then runs a forced push-then-pull so a manual retry is never swallowed by the backoff the user is trying to escape. The button was dead before this phase (`SyncState.idle` and `isPremium: false` in every host); it now reports the live engine through `syncHealthProvider` and the single entitlement derivation, with the host's own parameter still honoured as an override.
- **The 60-second throttle now means something.** `canSkipResumePull()` requires **all three**: a full pull has succeeded before, the **same Realtime generation is still subscribed** (so no disconnect, re-join, channel error or fresh join happened in between), and that pull is inside 60 s. Elapsed time alone never skips a pull — Broadcast is ephemeral, so uncertain continuity is always repaired through REST. A skipped resume still asks for a push-only run, so anything queued while the app was away leaves immediately and costs no request when the queue is empty. One deliberate reading is worth recording: the channel is **not** closed on `inactive` (a call, the app switcher, a system dialog) — only on `paused`/`hidden`/`detached`, which is what "backgrounded or the screen locked" means in the plan. Closing on `inactive` would break continuity on every trip to the switcher and make the throttle dead code.
- **Gap repair is the drain's own order, now wired to the reconnect.** Connectivity restoration and every resume reset the ladder, rejoin, then push pending work and force a per-table delta pull; health is recorded as successful only after that pull commits, together with the generation it committed under — the pair the throttle reads next time.
- **Tests:** 11 new — 9 in `test/sync/sync_health_test.dart` (the default ladder is the plan's six rungs and 3-failure threshold; a failed join waits on each rung before retrying, proven with a 100 ms/600 ms schedule so a busy loop would fail it; active retrying stops after the last rung and stays quiet; three failures pause sync and a success clears them; a lost channel counts as a failure while a re-join only ends the ladder, never claiming a sync success; `retryNow` resets everything and joins immediately; leaving the foreground stops the ladder and returning attempts immediately; losing the link stops it and regaining it re-joins) and 2 in `test/sync/sync_service_test.dart` (a successful pull makes a resume skippable, and a re-join — a new generation — makes the next resume pull all twelve tables again). **Protocol:** `flutter analyze` clean; `flutter test test/sync/` **93 passed**; full suite **1057 passed**. Version `0.0.317`.
- **Resume point:** Task 19 Phase 3 — three-way conflict reconciliation: the pure rebase verdict (disjoint fields rebase against the latest server version, overlapping or coupled fields stay for review), the `conflict_log.mergeable_conflict` column (Drift v9→v10 plus one Supabase migration), tombstone conflicts requiring an explicit rebase-onto-new-record or confirmed discard, the Review Conflict screen, the app-wide non-dismissible banner above the bottom navigation bar, and mirroring a resolution back to Supabase.

### 2026-09-30 — Task 19 Phase 1: a firm's edits now announce themselves, and a ping is only ever a hint — COMPLETE

- **The server half already existed, so this phase is client-only.** Task 16 Phase 3's `broadcast_row_change()` fires a `{table, id}` Broadcast ping from **all twelve** domain tables (including `court_categories`, which is the Addendum's requirement) to the row's private topic — `firm:{firm_id}` or `user:{user_id}` — and the `realtime_messages_read_own_topics` policy on `realtime.messages` is live on `lsthasywrdrhuuxnykau` (checked with `pg_policies`, not assumed). No migration, no ledger change, nothing applied to the database this phase.
- **One channel, notification-only, foreground-only.** `lib/sync/realtime_listener.dart` replaces the Part A `library;` stub: the app joins exactly one channel with `private: true`, listens for the trigger's `row_change` event, and hands the collected `{table, id}` pings to the sync service. It is opened on resume — and as soon as a signed-in, entitled account's profile arrives, because a cold start reaches `resumed` before the pull that carries it — and closed **immediately** on anything other than `resumed`, including `inactive`, so a backgrounded or locked app holds no socket. The channel is entitlement-gated like every other sync path: a free account never opens one (plan §6).
- **A ping is a hint, never an event log.** The service collects pings for a short window (two windows, the listener's and the service's, so back-to-back bursts still become one run), applies them under the same single in-flight slot a drain uses, and pulls each row through `PullWorker.pullRow` → the very `_applyRow` a paged delta pull uses. So a notification cannot bypass conflict handling, cannot overwrite queued local work, and — because `pullRow` deliberately does **not** advance the cursor — can never make the next delta pull skip a row. A failed ping pull is swallowed: the resume/reconnect delta pull is what repairs the gap.
- **A connection generation, not a timestamp, will decide the resume throttle.** Every transition into `subscribed` — the first join, an automatic re-join after a socket drop, a channel error that comes back — increments a monotonic generation, so continuity can be proven by equality rather than assumed by elapsed time. Phase 2 is what consumes it.
- **Two small additions to the transport and the pull.** `SyncTransport.fetchRow(table, id)` (PostgREST `.eq('id', …).maybeSingle()`, so an invisible row reads as null rather than as an error) and `PullWorker.pullRow(entityType, id)`; the four existing test transports gained the method. `syncPingFromBroadcast` accepts both the nested frame (`{type, event, payload:{table, id}}`) and a payload that is already the trigger's object, because the two have existed in different client versions.
- **Tests:** 13 new — 11 in `test/sync/realtime_listener_test.dart` with a hand-driven fake socket (topic joined once, a second start is a no-op, coalescing with duplicate collapse, a ping outside the twelve tables dropped, stop leaves the channel and drops pending pings, a re-join is generation 2, a denied join reports failed, no signed-in account means no channel, a stop during a join closes what it opened, and the two frame shapes plus three rejections for the decoder) and 2 in `test/sync/sync_service_test.dart` (a ping micro-pulls exactly its row — no full pull, the row applied, `last_successful_sync_at` stamped — and a ping for `profiles`/`user_preferences` is ignored). **Protocol:** `flutter analyze` clean; `flutter test test/sync/` **82 passed**. Version `0.0.316`.
- **Resume point:** Task 19 Phase 2 — connectivity and reconnection: exponential backoff 1s → 32s with a six-retry ceiling, the "Sync paused" header indicator after three failures (tapping forces a retry and resets the backoff), gap repair (push then a forced per-table pull) after every reconnection, and the continuity-aware 60 s resume throttle reading the generation this phase added.

### 2026-09-30 — Task 18's audit findings are fixed: the preview cannot travel alone, the drain compacts, and every successful drain stamps the clock — COMPLETE

- **Finding 1, the one real correctness defect.** `PushWorker._prepare()` carried a comment promising to drop a provisional history that was not attached to a mutation in the batch, and then returned it anyway: only a preview that had already been folded was skipped. Because the frozen-entity filter keys on the pair `(entity_type, entity_id)`, a preview (`case_history` + its own id) is **not** frozen when its parent case is — so after a case conflict, the next Quick Update's fresh case row was filtered out with the frozen entity while its preview went to the server **alone**, creating a history event for a next-date change that never applied there. The repaired retry then travelled without `provisional_history` and the RPC minted a fresh id, giving two events for one change: exactly what the "sole provisional event" rule exists to prevent.
- **The fix is one condition, and it is the intended one.** Every `case_history` **insert** is now skipped in `_prepare()` — the only history insert the client ever queues is a provisional preview — so a preview can travel only folded into the case update that produced it. It is **skipped, not deleted**: it stays `pending`, so once the case is repaired the pair travels together. The pairing is also guarded so one preview cannot be attached to two case mutations in the same batch (which would have hit a duplicate-key insert), and a user's history **edit** — an update, the Task 10 long-press path — still pushes on its own.
- **Finding 2: the matrix now runs on the ordinary drain.** `_drain()` calls `SyncQueueDao.compactPending()` before its first batch, so the Offline Compaction Matrix is applied before **every** push, not only inside `runBacklogPush()`. It reads `pending` rows only, so a frozen or blocked action is still untouchable. **One residual shape is left standing on purpose:** a *leading delete* run (delete, then an edit) is declined by `planQueueCompaction()` because Task 05's matrix chose not to guess which of the two the user meant; it therefore still travels in order, and the second mutation answers `tombstone` (a write against the row the batch's own delete just soft-deleted) — logged and frozen for review, never a silently wrong write. Extending the matrix is a Task 05 decision, so it is reported rather than taken.
- **Finding 3: a push-only drain is a successful sync too.** It now calls `_recordSyncHealth(..., probeClock: false)`, so `last_successful_sync_at` — the instant the 150-day offline check reads — is stamped after **every** successful drain. The server-clock probe and `clock_offset_ms` stay on the pull path, so a burst of edits still costs no extra round trip, and the skewed-clock notice keeps its ~2-minute threshold.
- **Tests: five new, and four of them were proven to fail first.** `test/sync/push_worker_test.dart` gains three (an unattached preview sends only the unrelated row — `attempted=1`, `sent=[note]` — and stays `pending`; a lone preview attempts nothing and burns no retry; a history edit is still pushed on its own), and `test/sync/sync_service_test.dart` gains two (the drain compacts a run into one mutation carrying the latest payload under the first row's identity; a push-only drain stamps `last_successful_sync_at` while pulling nothing). Each of the four behaviour tests was run once against the **reverted** code and each failed there — attempted 2 instead of 1, the lone preview not empty, two mutations in one batch, the stamp still null — so none of them is a decoration.
- **Protocol:** `flutter analyze` clean; full `flutter test` **1032 passed** (5 new); no SQL changed, so the Supabase ledger and its `--check` are untouched; `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.315`. `docs/task18_audit.md` records the fix and the one residual shape beside the findings it raised.
- **Resume point:** Task 18 is complete, audited and now repaired; nothing in Task 18 is outstanding except the two items the approved split explicitly left to Task 19 (the banners' rendering and the Realtime pings). The next work is **Task 19** — the Sync Status and conflict-review screen (three-way `conflict_log` review, `blocked` rows, the queue states), the clock-skew and permissions-changed banners off `SyncNotice`, the extended-offline lock reading `last_successful_sync_at`, and the branched logout — which will also want a production caller for `runBacklogPush()` on the free → premium activation signal.

### 2026-09-30 — Task 18 Phase 4: the backlog a free user accumulated now has somewhere to go — COMPLETE

- **One call, and the whole free period syncs.** `SyncService.runBacklogPush()` is the upgrade path of plan §2: compact, push FK-ordered micro-batches, then **full-pull** every table from the minimum sentinel. Task 21's activation signal is what calls it — no trigger is wired here, because the approved plan keeps that with Task 21 — and it returns a `BacklogOutcome` (compacted / pushed / conflicts / pulled / conflictPending / blocked / failure) that Sync Status will read. It runs under the same single-in-flight rule as every other drain, refuses to start with no client, offline or not entitled, and on a transport failure it keeps the queue and skips the pull, because the pull would fail on the same link.
- **The compaction is real, and the matrix is Task 05's.** `lib/sync/queue_compaction.dart` holds `planQueueCompaction()`, the pure Offline Compaction Matrix applied to a **run** of rows for one entity: the earliest row keeps the identity (`mutation_id`, `base_version`, `base_payload`, `pre_mutation_payload`), the latest payload wins, and the changed fields are unioned. `SyncQueueDao.compactPending()` groups the queue, applies that plan in one transaction, and returns how many rows it removed. It exists because the write path can accumulate rows — two inserts for one entity, or a delete followed by an edit — and pushing two mutations for one entity in one batch would make the second conflict against the version the first just produced. A delete followed by an edit is deliberately **left alone**: the matrix does not define that shape, and guessing would be worse than pushing it in order.
- **Ineligible rows are untouchable, and that is now pinned.** `compactPending()` reads only `pending` rows, and the worker skips frozen entities, so a `conflict_pending` row keeps its payload and stays for review while a `blocked` row keeps its state, its `retry_count` and its error message — never silently cleared and never force-retried. A `blocked` row joins the backlog the moment the user repairs it back to `pending` (Task 19's Sync Status), and a permanent rejection during the pass lands as `blocked` like any other.
- **Zero local data migration, demonstrated rather than asserted.** The last test writes a row through the real `SyncWriteHelper` carrying exactly what a repository supplies — `updated_at`, `synced_at`, `is_deleted`, `last_modified_by`, no `version` — and checks that the mutation that reaches the server carries that payload **unchanged** with a null `base_version` for a first insert. Nothing re-derives, rewrites or back-fills anything on the way out.
- **Tests:** 12 new in `test/sync/backlog_push_test.dart` — five pinning `planQueueCompaction` (insert+update keeps the insert with the latest payload and the union of fields, insert+delete cancels out, update+delete collapses to a delete, delete+update is left alone, a single row is untouched) and seven integration paths over a real `ProviderContainer` and an in-memory database: the compaction plus FK-ordered batch plus a 12-table sentinel pull that forgets a stale cursor, a pulled firm row landing on the freshly pulled device, a preserved `conflict_pending`, a `blocked` row never attempted and never retried, a permanent rejection staying visible, a transport failure keeping the queue and skipping the pull, and the Part A payload arriving unchanged. **Protocol:** `flutter analyze` clean; full `flutter test` **1027 passed**; supabase suite 74 passed; ledger `--check` clean; `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.314`.
- **Resume point:** Task 18's four phases are complete. What remains is the task file's own acceptance checklist, run item by item with the Addendum's items included — that is the next thing to do, and it is a report, not new code. Then Task 19 (Realtime pings, Sync Status and the conflict-review UI, the clock-skew and permissions banners, the extended-offline lock and the branched logout) reads what this task recorded: `SyncNotice`, `conflict_log` with its `server_payload`, the `blocked`/`conflict_pending` queue states, `last_successful_sync_at`, `clock_offset_ms`, and the per-table cursors.

### 2026-09-30 — Task 18 Phase 3: the other half of sync exists — a device now learns what its firm did — COMPLETE

- **The pull is one page at a time behind a per-table cursor.** `lib/sync/pull_worker.dart` walks the twelve domain tables in the same FK-safe order the push uses, and each table carries its own `(server_updated_at, id)` cursor in `sync_metadata` (`SyncCursor`, `syncCursorKey`, `cursor.<table>`), advanced **inside the same Drift transaction as the page it describes** — so a crash between the fetch and the commit can replay a page but can never skip one. The cursor is the raw `server_updated_at` string the server returned, never a re-formatted `DateTime`, and the filter is the plan's `server_updated_at > t OR (= t AND id > id)` built by the pure `syncPullFilter()` so the exact string is pinned by a test. Pages are requested ascending by both columns at 200 rows until a **short** page arrives; an empty page completes the table without inventing a device-clock cursor, and a page whose last row carries no readable cursor ends the table rather than looping forever on it. A full pull (initial sync, and Phase 4's post-upgrade pull) simply forgets the cursors first — `pullAll(fromSentinel: true)`.
- **Pull never overwrites work the server has not seen.** `PullWorker` takes the still-unpublished queue entries once per run (`getUnresolved()`, earliest entry first) and, for a pulled row that intersects one, writes a `conflict_log` record carrying the authoritative row as `server_payload`, the queued payload as `submitted_payload` and the queue's own base as `base_payload` (so Task 19's three-way review has all three), freezes the entity (`conflict_pending`) and leaves the local row exactly as the user left it. A pulled **tombstone** is the one case that always applies, because hiding a deleted record is the point of it; its conflict record still preserves the pending edit, and a `tombstone`-with-pending row is therefore hidden, not resurrected. An entity already frozen on a conflict is not re-logged on later pulls (a newer server row is still inside the delta), and a **`blocked`** entry is deliberately untouched — pulling must not relabel a permanent rejection as a version conflict or overwrite what the user is about to review in Sync Status.
- **A drain pushes first and pulls only when asked.** `SyncService` now runs push → pull → sync health, and `requestSync(pull: true)` is what asks for the pull; launch, resume and reconnect pass it, while a **local write burst only pushes** — otherwise every 2.5 s coalescing window would have cost thirteen requests instead of one. A mid-run pull request is remembered (`_pullWanted`) and honoured by the run that follows, and a backoff retry asks for one too, because whatever was missed while the link was down is repaired by the delta pull (Realtime is notification-only, Task 19).
- **Sync health is recorded on every successful pull.** `last_successful_sync_at` (the 150-day offline check reads it) and `clock_offset_ms` land in the durable `sync_metadata` table, as the Addendum requires, and a device more than two minutes off the server raises `SyncNotice.clockSkew` — the offset and the ARB copy (`sync_clockSkewWarning`) land here, while the banner itself stays with Task 19's Sync Status work, per the approved plan. The clock probe is best-effort: an unreadable server clock records nothing and cannot fail a sync that already succeeded.
- **The transport grew its second and third calls.** `SyncTransport.fetchPage(table, sinceTime, sinceId, limit)` pages one table over PostgREST (RLS scopes it to the caller's firm, so no firm id travels in the request), and `serverTime()` reads the `sync_server_time()` RPC applied in the server half of this phase. `decodeServerTime` reads both the scalar and the row form and returns null rather than guessed. One refactor came out of this: the conflict-log write was extracted to `sync_conflict_log.dart` (`writeConflictLog`), so the push worker's server-side conflict and the pull's local-side conflict cannot drift into writing different-shaped records.
- **Two defects, both caught by the new tests.** The pull's closure could not see `syncEntityForTable(table)` as non-null, so the binding lookup is now guarded on both the entity type and the binding; and the cursor write is a direct `target != null` check inside the transaction rather than a `!` on a boolean-derived value, which is what the analyzer's `unnecessary_non_null_assertion` warning was pointing at. On the test side, the conflict-log **mirror** is itself a queued row, so `queue.getAll().single` was counting two rows — the merge tests now filter by entity type.
- **Tests:** 17 new in `test/sync/pull_worker_test.dart` — the twelve-table walk, same-timestamp pagination across a page boundary (`a1,b2 → c3,d4 → e5`, exactly three requests), resuming from a stored cursor, an empty page inventing no cursor, the sentinel pull ignoring stored cursors, a crash inside the page transaction leaving the cursor and the rows untouched and replaying on the next run, a transport failure leaving every cursor and row alone, independent per-table cursors, the cannot-advance guard, and the five merge paths (pending edit preserved + all three payloads in the log, tombstone hidden with the edit kept, frozen not re-logged, `blocked` untouched, a resolved entry no longer blocking the server row) — plus the pure `syncPullFilter`, `decodeServerTime` and `SyncCursor` decoders. Five more in `test/sync/sync_service_test.dart`: a write-coalescing drain pushes but never pulls, a pull drain covers all twelve tables after the push and records `last_successful_sync_at`, a pulled page lands with its cursor, and clock skew warns past two minutes and stays silent (but still records) within it. **Protocol:** `flutter analyze` clean; full `flutter test` **1015 passed**; supabase suite 74 passed; ledger `--check` clean; `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.313`.
- **Resume point:** Task 18 Phase 4 — the initial backlog push on a free → premium upgrade: `SyncService.runBacklogPush()` compacting only eligible `pending` rows, preserving `conflict_pending`, surfacing `blocked` instead of clearing them, pushing FK-ordered micro-batches, then a full pull from the sentinel, with an integration test. Then the final acceptance checklist (including the Addendum) item by item.

### 2026-09-30 — Task 18 Phase 2: the queued rows now actually leave the device — one batch, one radio wake-up — COMPLETE

- **The stubs are gone.** `lib/sync/sync_service.dart` and `push_worker.dart` were Part A `library;` placeholders; they are now the real serialized FIFO engine. Four new files carry the shared pieces: `entity_tables.dart` (the `entity_type → table` allow-list mirroring the server's `sync_entity_table()`, the FK-safe dependency rank, and the snake↔camel + JSON decoders), `sync_table_bindings.dart` (one Drift read/write pair per entity, so the generic caller is a map lookup rather than a 12-arm switch), `sync_transport.dart` (`SyncTransport` over `push_changes`, with `decodePushResults` and a `mapSyncFailure` classifier), and `sync_trigger_scope.dart` (the four moments sync is asked to run).
- **One batch, one call, and the whole queue is drained.** `PushWorker.flushBatch()` loads the pushable rows, skips every entity frozen on a conflict (a **single** `getConflictPendingKeys()` query, not a per-row check), sorts by dependency rank then `created_at`, folds a case update's provisional history preview into that same mutation (so it is never pushed separately and never splits across a batch), marks `last_attempt_at` on every row immediately before the attempt (AGENTS.md Addendum), and sends one `push_changes` call. `SyncService` loops it up to 20 batches, so a backlog clears in one run.
- **The single-in-flight rule is synchronous, and that was a real fix.** The first version set `_inFlight` after `await _isEntitled()`, so two calls in the same microtask both became the runner and pushed twice; the slot is now claimed before any await, and a request that arrives mid-run sets `_queued` and is re-run once at the end. The queued re-run and the background retry timer both check `ref.mounted`, which the test caught as a disposed-ref error after teardown.
- **Every terminal result is applied locally.** Accepted → `markSynced` (version + `synced_at` only, never overwriting a newer queued edit) and the entry resolved; a returned authoritative `history` row replaces the local provisional preview and resolves its paired queue entry; cascade `children` are upserted and their queue rows resolved. Conflict/tombstone → a local `conflict_log` row **and** a queued mirror of it, the entity frozen (`freezeEntity`), and a tombstone additionally applied so the record hides. Permission rejection → rolled back from `pre_mutation_payload`, the entry resolved, and `SyncNotice.permissionsChanged` set. Everything else permanent → `blocked` with the server's message.
- **Retry policy is the plan's.** A retryable transport failure returns the whole batch to `pending` with a bumped `retry_count`; at the tenth it is `blocked`; a non-retryable failure blocks immediately; the backoff doubles from 30 s and caps at 30 m (`syncBackoffDelay`, pinned by test). The premium gate is the existing single derivation (`premiumAccessProvider`), and the worker never runs with no client, no entitlement or no link.
- **The four trigger moments are wired, not delegated.** `SyncTriggerScope` (mounted inside `AccountRefreshScope` in `app.dart`) requests a sync after the first frame (launch), on `AppLifecycleState.resumed`, on a false→true `isOnlineProvider` transition (reconnect), and on a **rise** in `syncQueuePendingCountProvider` — the pending-only count, so the worker's own `pending → pushing → resolved` churn does not look like new work. The service's 2.5 s `syncCoalescingWindow` turns a burst of edits into one radio wake-up. Realtime pings and the Sync Status UI remain Task 19's.
- **Two ARB strings landed, in English and Bengali:** `sync_permissionsChanged` (Title Case, per rule 11) and `sync_clockSkewWarning` (used in Phase 3), regenerated through `flutter gen-l10n`.
- **Tests:** 24 new in `test/sync/push_worker_test.dart` (dependency ordering, `last_attempt_at`, conflict freeze, accepted sync, the provisional-history fold, conflict log + mirror, tombstone hiding, permission rollback, block paths, the ten-failure cap, decoding, backoff) and 5 in `test/sync/sync_service_test.dart` (entitlement, offline, drain, single-in-flight, backoff window), all over an in-memory Drift database and a fake transport. **Protocol:** `flutter analyze` clean; full `flutter test` **992 passed**; `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.312`.
- **Resume point:** Task 18 Phase 3 — the delta pull: `pull_worker.dart`, per-table `(server_updated_at, id)` composite cursors in `sync_metadata`, push-then-pull on launch/resume, the three-way pull merge with `conflict_pending`, clock-skew detection, and per-device `last_successful_sync_at`.

### 2026-09-30 — Task 18 Phase 1: every queued mutation now has one server-side entry point, and it was proven by running it — COMPLETE

- **The gap this closes.** Every local write since Task 05 has been queued in `sync_queue` with nowhere to go. `supabase/migrations/20260930140000_push_changes.sql` is that destination: `push_changes(rows jsonb)`, the single atomic push pipeline of Sync Plan §1.1, applied to `lsthasywrdrhuuxnykau` through the applier and verified against the live project. It writes only the twelve push/pull domain tables — `sync_entity_table()` is the allow-list, so a pull-only cache, preference or server-managed table cannot be reached through it (AGENTS.md rule 4).
- **The contract, in one place.** The wire keys are the queue's own snake_case names (`mutation_id`, `entity_type`, `entity_id`, `operation`, `base_version`, `base_payload`, `changed_fields`, `payload`, `pre_mutation_payload`, plus an optional `provisional_history`). One outer transaction, one implicit savepoint per logical mutation (`begin … exception … end`), mutations applied in FK-safe dependency order (the six config tables, then `cases`, then anything that may reference one) and exactly one result per `mutation_id` returned **in input order**. A permanent per-mutation failure (permission, entitlement, validation) rolls back only its savepoint; any other error is infrastructure and aborts the whole RPC.
- **Correctness rules the plan names, implemented rather than assumed.** Optimistic concurrency: the row is locked `for update`, an update/delete is accepted only on an exact `base_version` match, the server assigns `version = current + 1` (1 on insert) and `last_modified_by = auth.uid()`, and a client-supplied `version`/`server_updated_at`/`last_modified_by` is stripped before the row is written. Idempotency: a `mutation_receipts` row is written for every terminal result in the same transaction, so a retry with the same canonical hash returns the original result and a reused id with a different hash answers `mutation_id_reused` and applies nothing. Tombstones: an update against a soft-deleted row returns `tombstone` with the current row, a delete against one returns `already_resolved`. Cascade: an accepted case delete soft-deletes its `case_history` children in the same savepoint, gives each a new version, and returns the changed children.
- **History: the RPC owns it, exactly as the Addendum words it.** The client's provisional `case_history` preview stays local and is never pushed on its own; a date-changing case update carries the preview's identity in `provisional_history`, and the RPC writes the authoritative event under that id with `source_mutation_id`, `base_case_version` and `result_case_version = new case version`, in the same savepoint. An update that changes anything else creates no history, and a rejected mutation creates none at all. The task file's Phase 1.3 (`next_date` only) is followed over plan §1.1.3's wider wording, per the Addendum.
- **Permission and entitlement are re-checked, not inherited from RLS.** `push_changes` is `SECURITY DEFINER`, so RLS does not apply inside it: `sync_can_write()` re-expresses the Phase 4 policy rules on the row that would be written (own or `can_edit_firm`, `can_delete_firm` for a delete transition, built-in reference rows refused, delete requests scoped to the requester or a reviewer), and `has_sync_entitlement()` derives premium from the caller's **or their firm's** `subscription_expiry + 48h` — never `subscription_status`. Rejections come back as stable machine-readable codes (`permission_denied`, `entitlement_required`, `version_conflict`, `not_found`, `validation_failed`).
- **Two defects only running it could find, and both were real.** The first version inserted `select *` from `jsonb_populate_record`, which reads NULL for an absent key instead of applying a column default (and defaults matter: `is_pinned`, `is_custom`, `is_deleted`); the second wrongly added a `created_at` fallback to the six reference tables, which have no such column, so the very first smoke batch answered `column "created_at" of relation "courts" does not exist` and then a foreign-key failure on the case. `supabase/tests/push_changes_smoke_test.sql` caught both. The insert now names only the columns the snapshot actually carries, and the fallback is guarded by a real column check. The version row was deleted and the file re-applied through the applier after each fix, so what is recorded is what ran (AGENTS.md rule 13).
- **Verified live, and self-contained.** `supabase/tests/push_changes_smoke_test.sql` creates its own auth users, firm and privileges and rolls the whole transaction back — no fixture survives. It exercises: a free account refused `entitlement_required` having written nothing; a batch listing a case before its court accepted in dependency order; the date-changing update writing the authoritative history, a retry changing nothing, and a reused id refused; a stale `base_version` returning `conflict` with the authoritative row; an associate without `can_edit` refused `permission_denied`; the delete cascade returning the changed child, then `tombstone` and `already_resolved`; and one result per mutation in input order, each with a receipt. It ends `SMOKE OK: push_changes behaves as specified.`
- **Protocol:** `flutter analyze` clean; ledger regenerated (19 migrations, `--check` clean); `test/supabase/schema_contract_test.dart` extended with eleven `push_changes` invariants and the two migration-count pins moved 18 → 19 (all 73 supabase tests green); `supabase/README.md` updated to 29 functions and 19 versions with the live evidence and defect 6; `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Version `0.0.311`.
- **Resume point:** Task 18 Phase 2 — replace the `lib/sync/sync_service.dart` and `push_worker.dart` stubs with the real serialized FIFO push worker: a `SyncTransport` boundary (real over `activeSupabaseClientProvider`, fake in tests), the shared entity/table map, dependency-then-`created_at` ordering, the 2–3 s micro-batch window into one `push_changes` call, the compaction reuse with conflict freezing, backoff/`blocked`, and every result path (accept, conflict/tombstone → `conflict_log` + `conflict_pending`, permission rollback from `pre_mutation_payload`). **Carried forward:** the launcher/resume and write-coalescing triggers land in Phase 2/3, Realtime and the Sync Status/conflict UI stay Task 19, and the clock-skew banner's rendering may belong there too.

### 2026-09-29 — The lawyer's own details now live on the account, so a re-install cannot ask for them again — COMPLETE

- **The gap: the Profile screen wrote to the device and nowhere else.** The five fields at Settings > Accounts & Profile > Profile — name, bar association, membership number, office address, mobile number — have been one `SharedPreferences` record since Task 12, so a new phone, a re-install or a cleared app showed five dashes. Worse, the sign-up screen's Bar Association, whose comment claimed it was "sent to the server", was stored locally and nothing else: `signUpIndividual` had no such argument, so even a brand-new account threw it away.
- **The server side is one migration and no new function.** `20260929130000_profiles_personal_details.sql` adds `email`, `bar_association`, `membership_no`, `office_address` and `mobile_no` to `profiles`, backfills the e-mail for existing rows, and grants UPDATE **column-wise for the caller's own row** — the shape `full_name` has had since Phase 3, with `profiles_update_self` already restricting the row. That matters twice: no `SECURITY DEFINER` was added (the project's open hardening item is exactly the count of those), and nothing here touches `sync_queue`, because `profiles` stays a pull-only cache with one documented self-service write now recorded as a *Resolved Design Clarification* in the plan. `email` is deliberately **not** granted — it is the login identity, owned by `auth.users`, and a client that could write it could misrepresent itself to every firm member who can read the profile. Two triggers keep it exact: `handle_new_user()` writes the address and reads the Bar Association from the sign-up metadata, and `handle_user_email_change()` follows an e-mail change on the auth record.
- **The client mirrors the record in both directions, with the user's two rules.** Save writes the local record first (unchanged) and then PATCHes the account's own row, best-effort; a save that could not reach the server leaves a **pending flag** that the launch/resume/reconnect refresh retries, so an edit made in airplane mode is not silently stranded on the device that typed it. On the way back a pull **fills blanks only** — a detail typed here is never overwritten by the account's copy — and a record this device already holds is handed over to an account that has none, which is what makes a Part A install's details survive its first sign-in.
- **One defect the tests caught in the first version, and it was a data-loss bug.** The retry ran *before* the pull, and a push sends the whole record — so a device with a blank `membership_no` would have pushed that blank over the account's value and there would have been nothing left to recover it from. The merge now runs first and the pending push sends the merged record, and the case is pinned as its own test ("a retried push carries the account's own values with it"). `test/features/profile_details_test.dart` holds 17 tests: the payload and row mapping, malformed columns reading as blank rather than as a guess, `hasDetails`/`hasBlankDetails`, the Profile screen's real Save button mirroring the record, an offline save still saved and owed, the blanks-only pull, the retry that clears the flag, an unreachable account leaving both copies alone, and no push at all before an account exists. `auth_signup_test.dart` now also asserts the Bar Association genuinely reaches the repository as metadata.
- **Verified live, twice, in rolled-back transactions.** The sign-up trigger writes `email` and the Bar Association from the metadata (and leaves the other three blank, while a sign-up without the metadata invents nothing); an e-mail change follows onto the profile; the holder can update those four columns on their own row; **another account's row is invisible** under RLS; and `email`, `role`, `firm_id` and `subscription_expiry` refuse the client — for `authenticated` and for `anon` alike. The catalogue confirms the four new grants are the only ones added, and `supabase/README.md` records the evidence.
- **Protocol:** `flutter analyze` clean; ledger regenerated (18 migrations, `--check` clean); seed `--check` clean; `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean; full `flutter test` **962 passed** (17 new). Version `0.0.310`.
- **Resume point:** unchanged from the entries below, plus one new owed item — the two live verification runs above belong with the other three as rolling-back `supabase/tests/*.sql` scripts. The next task is still Task 18 (the sync service and `push_changes`), which is where the queued rows finally get sent.

### 2026-09-29 — The device id now follows the account, so no local write can be owned by a UUID the server has never seen — COMPLETE

- **The gap, stated precisely.** Every local write was stamped by `LocalIdentityService.getId()` — a per-install `Uuid().v4()` — and adoption only fixed what already existed: it rewrote the 12 domain tables and the queue payloads to `auth.uid()`, then left the seam alone, so the very next row reverted to the placeholder. Two concrete costs: (a) the row and the payload it queues disagree with the account, and Task 18's `push_changes` would be refused by the server's own policy (`cases_insert_own … with check (user_id = auth.uid())`, `20260929103000_phase4_rls_core_tables.sql`); (b) the per-user preference keys drifted — adoption re-keyed them to the account and the next Settings save put the placeholder back, and a placeholder-keyed row cannot even be *inserted* server-side, because `user_preferences.user_id` is a foreign key to `auth.users`.
- **One new method closes it, because there was only ever one seam.** `LocalIdentityService.bindAccount(userId)` sets the cache and the stored value, and `adopt` calls it **after its transaction commits** and never on a refusal — so `getId()`, and through it `BaseLocalRepository.currentUserId`, `SyncWriteHelper.currentUserId` (all ~10 repositories), `accountCacheUserIdProvider`, `LocalSettingsRepository._userId` and the Add/Edit Case sheet's `case_notification_preferences` write all return the account id from that instant. No repository needed editing; the direct writers were correct all along and merely read the wrong value. `bindAccount` is deliberately **not** undone by sign-out: the rows on the device are still that account's work, and the account-switch refusal depends on the binding surviving. `clear()` is still there for Task 19's branched logout, and its doc now says so instead of implying it belongs to sign-out. The background notification isolate reads the same key through `peekId()`, so a scheduled alert now looks up the account's preference row rather than the placeholder's.
- **The preference merge is the interesting half, and the trap is a primary key.** `_rekeyPreferences` can no longer be a plain `UPDATE`: when both keys exist, moving one onto the other aborts the whole adoption with a constraint violation, taking the 12-table rewrite with it. Both tables now drop the **older** of the two rows — one symmetric `delete … exists` per direction, so either side can be the survivor — and then move whatever remains, matching `case_notification_preferences` per case rather than per user. The newer row wins because it holds the user's most recent choice, and the merge runs on **every** launch that finds a stray key, so the repair happens on an already-bound device too, not only on a fresh adoption.
- **Tests: 11 in the new `test/features/write_identity_test.dart`,** and the invariant each one pins is the same: one UUID. A row written after sign-in carries the Auth UUID and so does its queued payload (no placeholder anywhere in it); a row written before sign-in still carries the placeholder, because a Part A install has no other answer; `getId()`, `peekId()` and `accountCacheUserIdProvider` agree; the settings row and the per-case notification row both land under the account; sign-out keeps the binding; a refused switch binds nothing. Then the merges — the placeholder row moves, the account's newer row wins and the older is dropped, the placeholder's newer row wins when the dates are the other way round, per-case rows merge case by case, and the stray row is repaired on an already-bound device. Two of them failed on the first run and both were real: a `LateInitializationError` in a test that never called its harness's `setUp`, and the merge that only deleted in one direction.
- **The audit's ranked follow-up #2 is closed by this**, deliberately ahead of Task 18/19 rather than inside them, because "every local write names one account" is a property of the seam, not of the sync service — and `docs/task17_audit.md` now records it as closed with the mechanism, so it cannot be rediscovered as a defect. **One thing to be clear about:** `last_modified_by` on the *server* is still server-assigned by `push_changes` (Task 18); what this change guarantees is that the value this device *submits* is always the authenticated id, not that the trigger is written yet.
- **Protocol:** `flutter analyze` clean; ledger `--check` clean (no SQL changed); `python tool/record_version.py --check` clean. Full `flutter test` **945 passed** (11 new). Version `0.0.309`.
- **Resume point:** Task 17 remains complete and audited, with two of its five ranked follow-ups now closed. What is left is unchanged and mostly the user's: commit the three live verification runs as rolling-back `supabase/tests/*.sql` scripts, the branched logout flow (Task 19), and the dashboard work (Google provider + Web client ID, `{{ .Token }}` in the recovery template, Realtime Authorization) plus the two device passes. The next task is Task 18 (the sync service and `push_changes`), which is where the queued rows this change made server-acceptable finally get sent.

### 2026-09-29 — The auth links stop being black buttons, and Sign-up asks for the password twice — COMPLETE

- **The black chips were never the design.** The shared theme gives `TextButton` a black background with a white label (right for Settings), so every link on the auth screens was drawn as a filled button — including ones the mockups draw as plain text (`<a class="font-title-md text-secondary">Sign Up</a>`). The fix is one new shared widget, `AuthTextLink`: transparent background, `AppTypography.titleMd` (the system's bold, the mockup's `font-title-md`), underlined only where asked. It is deliberately not a theme change, because every other screen depends on the black `TextButton`. Two of the three requests therefore *restore* mockup fidelity rather than deviate from it.
- **Login:** "Forgot Password?" left the Password label row and now sits under the password box, right-aligned, as bold text with no background and no underline; the "Don't have an account? Sign Up" action is bold underlined text instead of a black button. **Reset Password:** "Back To Login" is the same plain bold underlined text (`AuthTextLink` directly, replacing a switch line with an empty prompt). **Sign-up:** "Already have an account? Login" gets the same treatment.
- **A required Confirm Password field was added after Password, with its own validation.** `authConfirmPasswordError` compares the two as typed — no trimming, because a password may end in a space and "they match" has to mean exactly that — and reports `Confirm your password.` for an empty box and `Both passwords must match.` for a mismatch, both before any round trip. Three new ARB keys in English and Bengali; the field shares the password field's show/hide state and carries no toggle of its own, since two controls for one boolean would be noise.
- **Tests:** 7 new — the mismatch and the empty confirmation both refused before the round trip, the switch line's underline/bold/transparency on Sign-up and on Login, "Forgot Password?" being *below* the password field with no underline, the moved link still routing to the reset screen, and "Back To Login" being plain text on the reset screen. `auth_signup_test.fillForm` moved to the new field order (the invite code is now field 5). `flutter analyze` clean; full `flutter test` **934 passed**. Version `0.0.308`.
- **Open, and deliberate:** the two mockups still show hover-underline links and no Confirm Password field, so `mockup_html_screens/settings_onlineSync_signup/code.html` no longer matches the shipped screen. Flagged rather than guessed at — either the mockups get updated to the app's decided look, or the Confirm Password field moves (its removal is one block plus two ARB keys). "Bold" was implemented as the design system's title weight (w600, the mockup's `font-title-md`); a heavier w700 is a one-line change if that is what was meant.

### 2026-09-29 — Task 17's last open Addendum item is closed: an abandoned onboarding is removed server-side, not merely left behind — COMPLETE

- **The gap was the *invoke* half, and it needed a server function.** The Addendum says an account whose join-firm onboarding is abandoned before redemption must be treated as never created; retryability was implemented, the removal was not, and it cannot live in the client because deleting an `auth.users` row is beyond anything `authenticated` may do. `supabase/migrations/20260929120000_abandon_provisional_account.sql` adds `abandon_provisional_account()` — `SECURITY DEFINER`, no arguments, granted to `authenticated` only.
- **The interesting part is not the delete, it is the guard, because the cost of a wrong deletion is a lawyer's account.** It refuses, naming the reason, on: a firm in `profiles.firm_id`, a role other than `individual`, a subscription; owning a firm, holding a `firm_members` row, having issued an invite; any authored row (cases, case history, notes, case references, delete requests, conflict records, custom reference data); a payment or approval, administrator rights, a sync receipt. Removal is then a **single statement**: `profiles`, `firm_members`, both preference tables, `invite_attempts` and `app_admins` all cascade from `auth.users`, and every reference that does not cascade is one the guard has already refused, so the delete cannot fail on a foreign key.
- **The client fires it only where abandonment is real, and never at the risk of a sign-out.** New `FirmRepository.abandonOnboarding()` returns true/false (`removed` vs `not_provisional` — a refusal is an answer, not an error) behind the same online guard as the owner-side primitives. `AuthViewModel.signOut()` calls it **before** ending the session, and only when an unredeemed invite code says this device has an unfinished join-firm onboarding — that code is discarded in the same breath, so the two facts cannot disagree. A failed or offline cleanup can never fail the sign-out (marked `ponytail:` with its ceiling — an offline sign-out leaves an empty provisional account behind — and its upgrade path, a retry on the next launch).
- **Verified by running it, against ten fixtures, in one rolled-back transaction.** Signed out → `unauthenticated`. A fresh account with nothing on it → `removed`, with its account, profile, per-user preferences and failed-attempt ledger all gone (cascade, proven by counting). Nine accounts that had been used for something each came back `not_provisional` with their own reason — firm ownership, a firm on the profile (via the real `ensure_firm`), firm membership, cases, notes, custom courts, administrator rights, a payment, a subscription — and **nine of ten accounts survived**. `authenticated` has EXECUTE, `anon`/`public` do not, and an `anon` call is refused. The transaction rolls back, so no fixture is left behind.
- **Tests and protocol:** 5 new in `test/features/invite_onboarding_test.dart` (the offline refusal, the call on sign-out, no call without an unfinished onboarding, and the two paths that must not break a sign-out: a server refusal and an unreachable server). Ledger regenerated into a `0.0.307` group with `--check` clean; the two migration-count pins moved 16 → 17; `supabase/README.md` now reports 17 versions, 23 functions and the live evidence; `docs/task17_audit.md`'s Addendum 3 is **PASS** and its first finding is closed. `flutter analyze` clean; full `flutter test` **927 passed**. Version `0.0.307`.
- **Resume point:** the audit's remaining ranked follow-ups — the domain write path's identity sweep, committing the three live verification runs as `supabase/tests/*.sql` scripts, the branched logout flow (Task 19), and the user's dashboard work (Google provider + Web client ID, `{{ .Token }}` in the recovery template, Realtime Authorization) with the two device passes.

### 2026-09-29 — Task 17 audited item by item: 21 of 21 phase items pass, three of four Addendum items, and the mockups were right — COMPLETE

- **The audit is a document, not a verdict line:** `docs/task17_audit.md` checks all five phases item by item, the four Addendum items and the five completion gates, with evidence from three independent sources — the source as written (services, ARB copy, and both auth screens compared **string by string** against the two mockups), the **live** project (catalogue queries plus three rolled-back behavioural runs: `ensure_firm` end to end, the full invite lifecycle, and the sign-up/`profiles` trigger path), and the suites (**110** Task 17 tests in nine files, the repository at **922 passed**, `flutter analyze` clean).
- **What the live runs added that no reading could.** The invite lifecycle was proven against the real server in one transaction: an owner creates a code under the `firm_invites_insert_owner` policy (what `createInvite` does), an associate redeems it typed **lower-case with stray spaces** and is `joined` with a member row and a relinked `profiles` row, the *same* code then answers `invalid_code` for a second account, a **revoked** code answers `invalid_code`, an associate reads **zero** rows from `firm_invites`, and `anon` is refused EXECUTE. `ensure_firm` was verified the same way, and the sign-up trigger was confirmed to create the `profiles` row every phase depends on.
- **The one failing Addendum item is the abandoned-onboarding cleanup.** Addendum 3's retryability half passes (a rejected code keeps the account, the form and the code; it clears only on success), but the *protected server cleanup* that treats an abandoned provisional account as never created **does not exist** — no migration implements it, and it cannot be done from the client, because deleting an `auth.users` row needs more than `authenticated` has. Closing it needs a guarded `SECURITY DEFINER`, which is a design decision, so it is reported rather than guessed at.
- **Eight further findings, each with its cost, and two of them closed by user ruling rather than by code.** Phase 2 item 3's claim that no login/sign-up mockup exists is **false** (both do, and the screens were built to them — user ruling); role and subscription state go in the details sheet because neither mockup has a row for them; the offline sentence lives on the still-stubbed Manage Associates screen because no mockup exists for it and its UI is Task 20's; `redeemInvite` is deliberately outside the online guard (an associate's failure already reads correctly as a network error); Phase 4's branched-logout half is Task 19's, and today's re-login path removes nothing at all; Google sign-in fails closed until `GOOGLE_WEB_CLIENT_ID` and the dashboard provider are set; the confirmation setting, the recovery template's `{{ .Token }}` and Realtime Authorization are dashboard-only and not readable from Postgres; and the domain write path still stamps the Part A placeholder id, which belongs with the sync service.
- **Resume point:** Task 17 is done and audited. The ranked follow-ups are in the audit — the abandoned-onboarding cleanup, the domain write path's identity sweep, committing the two verification runs as transportable `supabase/tests/*.sql` scripts, the branched logout flow (Task 19), and the user's dashboard work (Google provider + Web client ID, `{{ .Token }}` in the recovery template, Realtime Authorization) plus the two device passes. Version `0.0.306`.

### 2026-09-29 — Task 17 Phase 5: invite codes are 50-bit, the server sees only a hash, and firm administration says why it needs the internet — COMPLETE

- **The phase ships the primitives, not the screen, exactly as the task file draws the line** ("full Manage Associates UI arrives in Task 20"). `lib/features/accounts_and_profile/data/firm_repository.dart` gained the owner side: `createInvite()`, `revokeInvite(id)` and `ensureFirm(name)`, each one server call over a **pure function** — which is where the rules are pinned. Two things are deliberately *not* displayed here because no mockup exists for them yet: the plaintext code is returned once from `createInvite` for the Task 20 UI to show, and the offline sentence is rendered on the one firm-administration surface that exists today (the Manage Associates stub), which Task 20 replaces.
- **≥ 50 bits is a property of the alphabet and the length, not of "good" randomness.** `inviteCodeAlphabet` is Crockford Base32 — 32 symbols, and deliberately without `I`, `L`, `O`, `U`, the four characters a hand-read or phone-read code confuses with `1`, `0` and `V` — and `inviteCodeLength` is 10, so the space is `32^10 = 2^50` exactly. Each symbol is drawn independently from `Random.secure()`; tests pin the alphabet size, the exclusions, alphabet-only output, a thousand unique draws and reproducibility from a seeded source.
- **Only a hash ever leaves the device, and the hash is the server's own.** `inviteCodeHash` is `sha256(utf8(trim + uppercase))`, byte-identical to `join_firm`'s `sha256_hex(upper(btrim(code)))` — deliberately *without* the `O`→`0` "helpfulness" a code format invites, because a correction the server does not make would produce a code that never matches the row it was stored in. `inviteInsertRow` is the only object that reaches `firm_invites`: four keys, `code_hash` among them, **no plaintext in any shape**, which is what "insert only its SHA-256 hash" means in code. The plaintext lives in a single `GeneratedInvite` return whose `toString()` omits it, so an accidental interpolation into a log line cannot leak it — there is no cache, preference or log holding it anywhere.
- **A used code and a revoked one are indistinguishable on purpose.** `parseInviteRedemption` is the extracted, testable mapping: `join_firm` only matches `used_by is null and revoked = false`, so both arrive as `invalid_code` → `AuthFailureKind.invalidCode`, and the tests assert exactly that, because letting a client tell them apart would let a stranger probe which codes once existed.
- **Online-only is checked before the request, not inferred from its failure.** `_requireOnline()` runs first and raises the new `AuthFailureKind.firmAdminOffline`, whose sentence — *"Connect to the internet to manage your firm."*, in both ARB files — is the copy the task asks for rather than a generic connection message. The three owner-side calls are guarded; **redemption deliberately is not**, because the sign-up screen's existing network copy already reads correctly for a joining associate, and its failure is classified from the transport. Unknown connectivity counts as online, the same choice Phase 4 made. The Manage Associates surface shows the sentence when offline and nothing new when online.
- **The migration's first real execution found a fifth defect, and only running it could.** `ensure_firm` used one `v_now` bigint for `firms.created_at`, which is a `timestamptz` server instant, as well as for `firm_members.synced_at`/`permissions_synced_at`, which are client-facing epoch ms; the smoke run answered *"column created_at is of type timestamp with time zone but expression is of type bigint"*. `firms.created_at` now takes its column default. Because AGENTS.md rule 13 records what actually ran, the version row was deleted and the file re-applied through the applier, then the ledger regenerated.
- **`ensure_firm` is verified live, not merely applied.** In one rolled-back transaction: signed out it returns `{"status":"unauthenticated"}`; an empty name raises *"a firm name is required"* having created nothing; a real call returns `ready`, relinks `profiles.firm_id`/`role='owner'`, creates the firm with the caller as owner and upserts the owner's `firm_members` row with `can_add`/`can_edit`/`can_delete` true and both stamps set; a second call returns the same firm id (one firm, no duplicate member row); a different name renames it. `anon` and `public` are refused the EXECUTE grant, `authenticated` has it. `firms`/`profiles`/`firm_members` are untouched afterwards.
- **Tests:** 15 new in `test/features/invite_onboarding_test.dart` (the entropy and alphabet rules, the server-identical hash, the hash-only insert row, a code-free `toString`, the joined/already-member/invalid-code mapping, all three offline refusals against a client pointed at a closed port, the ARB sentence, and the offline surface widget), and `FakeFirmRepository` in `test/features/auth_test_support.dart` implements the three new methods so the existing suites drive the wider interface. The join path itself (*a valid code joins; a used or revoked code is refused and keeps onboarding retryable*) was already pinned end-to-end by Phase 2's sign-up suite. Ledger regenerated into its own `0.0.305` group with `--check` clean; the two migration-count pins moved 15 → 16; `supabase/README.md` now says 16 recorded versions, 22 functions and 63 supabase tests, and records defect 5. `flutter analyze` clean; full `flutter test` **922 passed**. Version `0.0.305`.
- **Resume point:** Task 17's five phases are complete.
